Modular operads, iterated distributive laws and a nerve theorem for circuit algebras Thanks: The author acknowledges the support of Australian Research Council grants DP160101519 and FT160100393.
Sophie Raynor

Abstract
. Circuit algebras are a symmetric version of Jones’s planar algebras. They originated in quantum topology as a framework for encoding virtual crossings. This paper extends existing results for modular operads to construct a graphical calculus and monad for general circuit algebras and prove an abstract nerve theorem. The proof relies on a subtle interplay between distributive laws and abstract nerve theory, and provides extra insights into the underlying structures. Oriented circuit algebras are equivalent to wheeled props and specialisations of the results to wheeled props follow as straightforward corollaries.



1.
Introduction

Circuit algebras are a symmetric version of Jones’s planar algebras, introduced in
[
3
]
as a framework for studying finite-type invariants of virtual knotted objects (see also
[
12
,
25
,
50
]
).
They are graded symmetric monoids equipped with a unary contraction operation satisfying suitable associativity and distributivity axioms. These axioms imply that circuit algebras may be equivalently described as algebras for an operad of wiring diagrams, as symmetric monoidal functors from a category of Brauer diagrams, or as modular operads in a monoid subcategory of symmetric species
[
42
]
.

Besides quantum topology, these structures arise in many areas of mathematics, including invariant theory
[
15
,
42
]
and string theory
[
45
]
. In particular, oriented circuit algebras are equivalent to wheeled props
[
13
,
42
]
which govern algebraic structures
with trace such as those that appear in contexts including Batalin–Vilkovisky quantisation and deformation theory (see e.g.,
[
15
,
38
,
36
]
).

This paper provides a detailed technical account of circuit algebra combinatorics. Building on analogous results for modular operads
[
41
]
, a monad and graphical calculus for circuit algebras is constructed and a nerve theorem in the style of Weber
[
6
,
52
]
is proved.

Theorem 1.1

(
Theorem

7.9
)
.

The category
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}
of circuit algebras is equivalent to the Eilenberg-Moore (EM) category of algebras for a monad
\mathbb{O}
on Joyal and Kock’s graphical species category
\mathsf{GS}

[
27
]
.

Let
\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{C})
denote the category of presheaves (contravariant
\mathsf{Set}
-valued functors) on a category
\mathsf{C}
.

Theorem 1.2

(
Theorem

8.4
)
.

There is a full subcategory
{\Xi^{\times}}\hookrightarrow{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}
, whose objects are undirected graphs. The nerve functor
N\colon{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}\to\mathsf{psh}({\Xi^{\times}})
induced by the inclusion
{\Xi^{\times}}\hookrightarrow{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}
is fully faithful.

A presheaf
P
on
{\Xi^{\times}}
is equivalent to the nerve of a circuit algebra if and only if it is “Segal”.

Put simply, the graphs encode ways of combining circuit algebra operations, and the “Segal condition” says that complex combinations of these operations are completely determined by their constituent parts (see
Section

2.3
and e.g.,
[
21
]
).

These theorems apply simultaneously to a very general class of circuit algebras that includes (non)oriented and coloured versions. A monad and nerve theorem for wheeled props, in terms of a category of directed graphs, follow as immediate corollaries (see
Corollary

8.6
).

The proof of
Theorem

1.2
uses Weber’s abstract nerve theory
[
6
,
52
]
. However, unlike the nerve theorems for categories and operads described in the examples of
[
52
, Section 4]
, the Segal condition for circuit algebras in
Theorem

8.4
(as with the Segal condition for modular operads in
[
41
, Theorem 8.2]
) cannot be directly obtained with Weber’s machinery: while Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
imply that the circuit algebra monad
\mathbb{O}
is “nervous” in the sense of
[
7
]
, it does not “have arities” in the sense of
[
6
,
52
]
.

In
[
41
, Introduction & Section 6]
, I explained how the so-called “problem of loops”, that is related to contractions of modular operadic units (the unit trace), means that the monad for unital modular operads does not admit a straightforward description in terms of the “graph-substitution” notion that underlies analogous constructions for operads
[
39
]
or prop(erad)s
[
22
,
36
,
31
]
. As circuit algebras are a special case of modular operads (
Section

3
and
[
42
]
), this is also an issue for constructing the monad
\mathbb{O}
of
Theorem

1.1
. Crucially, to obtain the Weber-style nerve theorem
Theorem

1.2
, there must be a fully faithful embedding of the graphical category
{\Xi^{\times}}
into the category
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}
of circuit algebras. This implies that the unit trace is encoded by a map from the graph consisting of a single, isolated (degree 0) vertex to the exceptional edge with no vertices (see
Remark

4.25
and
[
41
, Introduction, Figure 2, and Sections 6-8]
) and not by a map to an exceptional nodeless loop (as in e.g.,
[
23
,
24
,
49
]
).

The central principle underlying this work (and
[
41
]
) is that, under certain conditions, it is possible to build up a description of complicated algebraic structures by suitably combining simpler ones. In particular, if the simpler structures are governed by monads, we may be able to reframe this as a problem of “composing” these monads. Distributive laws
[
5
]
tell us whether and how monads can be composed and how they interact in the composite. Iterated distributive laws
[
10
]
describe conditions under which multiple monads may be composed.

In
[
41
, Section 7]
, the modular operad monad was obtained as a composition
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
, via a distributive law, of monads
\mathbb{T}
(governing composition) and
\mathbb{D}
(governing units) on
\mathsf{GS}
. For circuit algebras (
Section

7.2
), it is even necessary to extend the construction in
[
41
]
to include a third monad
\mathbb{L}
(governing the monoidal structure) and iterated distributive laws.

Theorem 1.3

(
Proposition

7.8
&
Theorem

7.9
)
.

There is a triple of monads
\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{T}
on
\mathsf{GS}
and iterated distributive laws between them such that the induced composite
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
is the circuit algebra monad
\mathbb{O}
from
Theorem

1.1
.

The decomposition in
Theorem

1.3
is crucial to the proof of
Theorem

1.2
. Weber’s nerve machinery
[
6
,
52
]
relies on a monad
\mathbb{M}
on a category
\mathsf{C}
preserving certain colimits in some proper subcategory
\mathsf{D}\subsetneq\mathsf{C}
, that is said to
provide arities
for
\mathbb{M}
, and this is only satisfied under very special conditions. However, if
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1}
is a composite monad, then each subset of
\{\mathbb{M}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{M}_{n}\}
describes a monad on
\mathsf{C}
and we may consider whether a certain extension (and/or lift) of this monad, if it exists, has arities (
Section

2.4
). In particular,
Theorem

1.2
is proved by showing that
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{T}
admits an extension to a monad with arities on the category
\mathsf{GS}^{\mathbb{D}}
of algebras for
\mathbb{D}
.

The iterated distributive laws used to prove Theorems
1.1
-
1.3
illuminate the graphical combinatorics of circuit algebras, and dovetail nicely with variations of circuit algebras that appear in the literature: Algebras for
\mathbb{L}
are monoids in
\mathsf{GS}
(
Remark

3.20
) so
Theorem

1.3
implies that circuit algebras are equivalent to modular operads (
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
-algebras) in the monoid subcategory of
\mathsf{GS}
. Moreover, algebras for the monad
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{T}
are nonunital circuit algebras, whose underlying modular operadic multiplication is not required to have a multiplicative unit (
Proposition

5.14
and
Lemma

7.4
). By
Section

5
, these are symmetric monoidal functors from subcategories of “downward” Brauer diagrams
[
44
]
(see also
[
42
]
and
[
32
,
47
]
).
In particular, these structures have applications to the representation theory of the infinite dimensional orthogonal, symplectic and general linear groups (see
[
44
]
and Sections 3.3 & 6.2 of
[
42
]
). These relationships are explored in the companion paper
[
42
]
. A partial summary is provided in
[
42
, Table 1]
.

This paper aligns with a larger body of recent works aimed at describing the underlying combinatorics of operad-like structures, such as cyclic and modular operads
[
17
,
20
,
23
,
24
,
29
,
41
,
47
,
51
]
, properads and props
[
22
,
30
,
46
]
. Bek McCann and I are currently using an iterated distributive law approach to provide an operadic description of compact closed categories. A key motivation for much of this research is to find models that characterise generalised operads and their algebras
up-to-homotopy
. The present work is no exception.

Recently, Dancso, Halacheva and Robertson have used circuit algebras to relate the graded Kashiwara-Vergne and
graded Grothendieck-Teichmüller groups,
\mathrm{KRV}
and
\mathrm{GRT}

[
14
]
. However, associativity of the circuit algebra operations is not preserved under passage to pro-unipotent completion and, to relate the ungraded groups
\mathrm{GT}
and
\mathrm{KV}
directly, it is necessary to weaken the circuit algebra axioms (see
[
14
, Remark 1.1]
).

Theorem

1.2
characterises circuit algebras via a
strong
Segal condition on graphical presheaves. So, it is natural to define homotopy circuit algebras as (simplicial set valued) presheaves that satisfy a weakened Segal condition. Indeed,
[
24
]
and
[
41
]
described models for homotopy modular operads – using different methods and slightly different graphical categories – as weak Segal presheaves. Unfortunately, neither of these results can be generalised straightforwardly to obtain a model structure on
\mathsf{psh}({\Xi^{\times}})
. It is for future work to explore whether such a model structure exists, or if the weak Segal condition is sufficient to describe a practical homotopy theory for circuit algebras.

The nonunital case, which avoids the problem of loops, is better understood: Stoeckl
[
46
]
has constructed a model for nonunital homotopy wheeled props, building on Ward’s proof that the operad for nonunital modular operads is Koszul
[
51
]
, and Kaufmann and Ward
[
29
]
have shown that the operad governing (nonoriented) nonunital circuit algebras (there called “Schwarz modular operads”) is Koszul. It would be interesting to compare the model for nonunital homotopy circuit algebras so-obtained with the Segal characterisation suggested by the nonunital case of
Theorem

1.2
. It is not expected that these results can be extended to unital circuit algebras. However, the iterated distributive law of
Theorem

1.3
together with Markl’s notion of distributive laws for operads in
[
37
]
– used in
[
29
]
to prove that the (monochrome) nonunital circuit algebra operad is Koszul – gives a possible approach for exploring Koszulity of the operad for unital circuit algebras. (See also
Remark

7.12
and
[
42
, Introduction]
.) Batanin and Berger’s theory of
tame polynomial monads

[
4
]
, together with the distributive law decomposition of the circuit algebra monad, could also shed light on the homotopy theory of unital circuit algebras.

Finally, a word on potential applications of this work outside mathematics. My own interest in circuit algebras, modular operads, and other operadic structures governed by graphs was originally motivated by questions involving complex networks with structure at multiple scales: Given a circuit algebra
A
, the bar construction for
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
and
A
may be viewed as a rulebook for
zooming in and out
of networks decorated by
A
. Indeed, there is an increasing body of work exploring this operadic (or monadic) approach in applications to fields such as systems engineering, dynamical systems, software engineering and neuroscience (see e.g.,
[
40
]
). Just as they provide the main difficulty for constructing the circuit algebra monad, cycles – or “feedback loops” – are the drivers of complexity in many real world networks (see e.g.,
[
18
,
19
]
). The approach of this work (and
[
41
]
) highlights the combinatorial structure of loops and may therefore shed light on the properties of feedback loops as they appear in diverse applications.

1.1.
Details and overview

Section

2
provides a relatively informal introduction to iterated distributive law and the abstract nerve machinery of
[
6
]
, on which the proofs of Theorems
1.1
-
1.3
are built, as well as some technical remarks on the relationship of this work to the extended algebraic patterns of
[
11
]
and extensions of strongly cartesian monads described in
[
6
, Proposition 4.4]
. Most of this section should be accessible to readers with just a basic knowledge of category theory.

In
Section

3
, circuit algebras and modular operads are introduced as
\mathsf{V}
-valued presheaves over
\mathsf{GS}
that satisfy certain axioms. It is important to note that the companion paper
[
42
]
and e.g.,
[
3
,
25
,
14
,
23
,
24
]
are concerned with circuit algebras (and modular operads) “enriched” in a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathsf{X},\otimes,I)
. From this perspective, different flavours of enriched circuit algebras are described as algebras over different operads. By contrast, this paper gives constructions “internal” to a category
\mathsf{V}
with sufficient (co)limits. The sets of colours (or generating objects) in the enriched version are replaced by “object objects” (palettes) in the internal version. This enables all (internal) circuit algebras to be obtained as algebras for a single monad. Of course, the two definitions are equivalent when
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{V}
is the category of sets.

The technical work of the paper starts at
Section

4
which is largely a review of basic graph constructions, using the formalism of Joyal and Kock’s “(Feynman) graphs”
[
27
]
(see also
[
23
,
24
,
41
]
), and explains the relationship with Brauer diagrams and wiring diagrams in terms of which circuit algebras are usually defined
[
3
,
13
,
14
,
42
]
.

A monad
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
for nonunital circuit algebras is constructed in
Section

5
. This is almost identical to the monad discussed by Kock in
[
31
]
and very similar to the nonunital modular operad monad
\mathbb{T}
constructed in
[
41
]
.
Section

6
reviews the construction in
[
41
]
of the modular operad monad
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
by composing
\mathbb{T}
with a unit monad
\mathbb{D}
, and gives an explicit description of the monad
\mathbb{T}_{*}
, that extends
\mathbb{T}
to the EM category of algebras for
\mathbb{D}
,
\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}
.

Despite the similarities between
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
and
\mathbb{T}
, the monad for unital circuit algebras cannot be obtained directly from a distributive law involving
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
and
\mathbb{D}
.
Instead, it is necessary to supplement the construction of
[
41
]
by observing that
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}=\mathbb{L}\mathbb{T}
is, itself a composite monad.
This happens in
Section

7
, in which an iterated distributive law is described and it is shown circuit algebras are equivalent to algebras for the resulting composite
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
of three monads on
\mathsf{GS}
.

Finally,
Section

8
contains a detailed description of the graphical category
{\Xi^{\times}}
for circuit algebras, and this leads easily to the proof of the nerve theorem (
Theorem

1.2
). The monad
\mathbb{L}
lifts to
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
algebras and
\mathbb{T}
extends to
\mathbb{D}
algebras. Hence, there is a “lifted” monad
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}\mathbb{T}_{*}
on
\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}
whose algebras are circuit algebras.
Theorem

1.2
is then implied by the observation that
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}\mathbb{T}_{*}
has arities on
\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}
and hence
satisfies the conditions of the abstract nerve machinery of
[
6
]
. Three different proofs of this theorem are discussed (c.f., Proof of
Theorem

8.4
and Remarks
8.17
and
8.18
).

2.
Distributive laws and abstract nerve theory

The main result of this work is the construction of a monad (
Theorem

7.9
) and a graphical category (
Section

8.3
) and nerve theorem (
Theorem

8.4
) for circuit algebras.

Theorem

8.4
is proved using the machinery of abstract nerve theory
[
6
]
, in combination with iterated distributive laws
[
5
,
10
]
.

Given the quantum topology origins of circuit algebras
[
3
,
14
]
and their connections with Brauer categories and representation theory
[
15
,
34
,
42
,
44
]
, this overview is aimed at a diverse audience including topologists, representation theorists and category theorists, and is intended to provide an accessible explanation of these formal concepts and how they interact in the proof of
Theorem

8.4
, as well as to establish notation and terminology for what follows.

More technical details are included as remarks, and I refer those interested in further details on the general theory to
[
6
,
52
]
, for abstract nerve theory, and
[
5
,
10
]
, for (iterated) distributive laws.

2.1.
Iterated distributive laws

This paper applies the general philosophy that, to understand something difficult, it is often helpful to break it up into pieces and instead study these simpler components and their local interactions. Monads are categorical devices that are often used to encode algebraic structures. In general, monads do not compose. But, in some cases, it may be possible to study a complicated algebraic structure via interactions between monads for simpler structures.

A monad
\mathbb{M}=(M,\mu^{\mathbb{M}},\eta^{\mathbb{M}})
on a category
\mathsf{C}
is given by an endofunctor
M\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}
, together with natural transformations
\mu^{\mathbb{M}}\colon M^{2}\Rightarrow M
(the
monadic multiplication
) and
\eta^{\mathbb{M}}\colon\mathrm{id}_{\mathsf{Cat}}\Rightarrow M
(the
monadic unit
) that satisfy axioms making
\mathbb{M}
into an associative monoid in the category of endofunctors on
\mathsf{C}
. (See, e.g.,
[
35
, Chapter VI]
, for detailed review of the general theory of monads and their algebras.)

An algebra
(c,\theta)
for
\mathbb{M}
consists of an object
c
of
\mathsf{C}
, and a morphism
\theta\colon Mc\to c
satisfying two axioms that assert the compatibility of
\theta
with
\mu^{\mathbb{M}}
and
\eta^{\mathbb{M}}
. Algebras are the objects of the
Eilenberg-Moore (EM) category
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
of algebras for
\mathbb{M}
, whose morphisms
(c,\theta)\to(d,\phi)
are given by morphisms
f\in\mathsf{C}(c,d)
such that
\phi\circ Mf=f\circ\theta
. The monad
\mathbb{M}
induces free-forgetful adjoint functors
\mathsf{C}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
where the left adjoint
\mathsf{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
is given by
c\mapsto(Mc,\mu^{\mathbb{M}}(c))
.

Let
\mathbb{M}=(M,\mu^{\mathbb{M}},\eta^{\mathbb{M}})
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=({M^{\prime}},\mu^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}},\eta^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}})
be monads on a category
\mathsf{C}
. In general, the composite functor
MM^{\prime}
does not carry a monad structure, since the data of
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is not sufficient to define a multiplication
(MM^{\prime})^{2}=MM^{\prime}MM^{\prime}\Rightarrow MM^{\prime}
.
Observe, however, that any natural transformation
\lambda\colon{M^{\prime}}M\Rightarrow M{M^{\prime}}
induces a natural transformation
\mu_{\lambda}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(\mu^{\mathbb{M}}\mu^{\mathbb{{M^{\prime}}}})\circ(M\lambda M^{\prime})\colon(M{M^{\prime}})^{2}\Rightarrow M{M^{\prime}}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
distributive law
for
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is a natural transformation
\lambda\colon{M^{\prime}}M\Rightarrow M{M^{\prime}}
such that the triple
(M{M^{\prime}},\mu_{\lambda},\eta^{\mathbb{M}}\eta^{\mathbb{{M^{\prime}}}})
defines a monad
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{{M^{\prime}}}
on
\mathsf{C}
.

Distributive laws were introduced by Beck
[
5
]
in terms of four axioms encoding their interactions with the monadic multiplications and units.

Let
\lambda\colon{M^{\prime}}M\Rightarrow M{M^{\prime}}
be a distributive law between monads
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
on
\mathsf{C}
. If
(c,\theta)
is an
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
algebra, then
c
admits an
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
algebra structure
\theta\circ\eta^{\mathbb{M}}M^{\prime}\colon M^{\prime}c\to MM^{\prime}c\to c
and an
\mathbb{M}
algebra structure
\theta\circ\lambda\circ M\eta^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\colon Mc\to M^{\prime}Mc\to MM^{\prime}c\to c
. In particular, the induced functor
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\to\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}
has a left adjoint and the monad
\mathbb{M}
lifts
to a monad on
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}
whose EM category of algebras is isomorphic to
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}
(see
[
5
, Section 3]
). Indeed, the existence of a functorial lift of
\mathbb{M}
to
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is equivalent to the existence of a distributive law
\lambda\colon{M^{\prime}}M\Rightarrow M{M^{\prime}}
(see e.g.
[
10
]
).

In general, the functor
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\to\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
does not have a left adjoint and so the monad
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
does not extend to a monad on
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
. However, in case the left adjoint does exist (in which case it is described by a certain right Kan extension
[
16
,
48
]
) and the counit of the adjunction is an isomorphism, then
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
may be extended to a monad
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}_{*}
on
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
such that
(\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}})^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}_{*}}\cong\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}
, and the following square of adjunctions commutes:

(2.2)

\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptsize{\top}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdash}
\textstyle{\vdash}
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptsize{\top}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Example 2.3
.

The category of small categories is equivalent to the EM category of algebras for the category monad on the category of directed graphs. This sends a directed graph
G
to the directed graph
\widetilde{G}
with the same vertices, and – for each directed path (of length
n\geq 0
) from
v
to
w
in
G
– a directed edge
v\leadsto w
in
\widetilde{G}
.

This monad may be obtained as a composite of the
semi-category monad
– which governs associative composition and assigns to each directed graph
G
the graph with the same vertices as
G
and edges corresponding to non-trivial directed paths (of length
n>0
) in
G
– and the
reflexive graph monad
that adjoins a distinguished loop at each vertex of a directed graph. The corresponding distributive law encodes the property that the adjoined loops provide identities for the (semi-categorical) composition.

Example 2.4
.

Similarly, the monad for modular operads on the category
\mathsf{GS}
of graphical species (see
Definition

3.2
) is obtained in
[
41
]
, as a composite
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
: the monad
\mathbb{T}
governs the composition structure (multiplication and contraction), and the monad
\mathbb{D}
adjoins distinguished elements that encode the combinatorics of the multiplicative unit. The distributive law ensures that the distinguished elements provide units for the multiplication (see also
Section

6
).

Let
\mathbb{M}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{M}_{n}
be an
n
-tuple of monads on a category
\mathsf{C}
.
If there are distributive laws
\lambda_{i,j}:M_{i}M_{j}\Rightarrow M_{j}M_{i}
(defined for all pairs
1\leq i<j\leq n
) such that, for each triple
1\leq i<j<k\leq n
, the corresponding triple of monads and distributive laws satisfies the so-called
Yang-Baxter
conditions

\lambda_{j,k}M_{i}\circ M_{j}\lambda_{i,k}\circ\lambda_{i,j}M_{k}=M_{k}\lambda_{i,j}\circ\lambda_{i,k}M_{j}\circ M_{i}\lambda_{j,k}\colon M_{i}M_{j}M_{k}\Rightarrow M_{k}M_{j}M_{i},

then, by
[
10
]
,
\mathbb{M}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{M}_{n}
may be composed to obtain a monad
\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1}
on
\mathsf{C}
.
It follows that, for each
1\leq i<n
, there are composable monads
\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{i+1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{i}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1}
on
\mathsf{C}
, and
\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{i+1}
lifts to a monad
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{(i+1)}
on the EM category
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{i}\dots\mathbb{M}_{i+1}}
.

2.2.
Presheaves, slice categories and nerve functors

Let
\mathsf{V}
be a category and let
\mathsf{C}
be an essentially small category. An
\mathsf{V}
-valued
\mathsf{C}
-presheaf
is a functor
S\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{V}
. The corresponding functor category is denoted by
\mathsf{psh_{\mathsf{V}}}(\mathsf{C})
.
If
\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{Set}
is the category of sets and all set maps, then we will write
\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{C})\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{psh_{\mathsf{V}}}(\mathsf{C})
.

The category of elements
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{C}}(P)
of a
\mathsf{Set}
-valued presheaf
P\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
is defined as follows:

Definition 2.5
.

Objects of
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{C}}(P)
are pairs
(c,x)
– called
elements of
P
– where
c
is an object of
\mathsf{C}
and
x\in P(c)
. Morphisms
(c,x)\to(d,y)

are given by morphisms
f\in\mathsf{C}(c,d)
such that
P(f)(y)=x
.

A functor
\iota\colon\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
induces a presheaf
\iota^{*}\mathsf{C}(-,c)
,
d\mapsto\mathsf{C}(\iota(d),c)
on
\mathsf{D}
. For all
c\in\mathsf{C}
, the category
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{D}}(\iota^{*}\mathsf{C}(-,c))
is called the
slice category of
\mathsf{D}
over
c
and is denoted by
\iota/c
, or simply
\mathsf{D}/c
.

The
slice category of
\mathsf{D}
under
c
, denoted by
c/\mathsf{D}
(or
c/\iota
) is the category
\iota^{\mathrm{op}}/c
whose objects are morphisms
f\in\mathsf{C}(c,\iota(-))
and whose morphisms
(d,f)\to(d^{\prime},f)
are given by
g\in\mathsf{D}(d,d^{\prime})
such that
\iota(g)\circ g=f^{\prime}\in\mathsf{C}(c,\iota(d^{\prime}))
.

A presheaf
P\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
is
represented
by some
c\in\mathsf{C}
if
P=\mathsf{C}(-,c)
. In this case,
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{C}}(P)
is precisely the
slice category

\mathsf{C}/c
whose objects are pairs
(d,f)
where
f\in\mathsf{C}(d,c)
, and morphisms
(d,f)\to(d^{\prime},f^{\prime})
are commuting triangles in
\mathsf{C}
:

\textstyle{d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{d^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{c}

In particular, the
Yoneda embedding

\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{C}),c\mapsto\mathsf{C}(-,c)
induces a canonical isomorphism
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{C}}(P)\cong\mathsf{C}/P
for all presheaves
P\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
. These categories will be identified in this work.

Definition 2.6
.

The
nerve functor

N=N(F)\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D})
associated to a functor
F\colon\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
is given by
N(c)(d)=\mathsf{C}(Fd,c)
for all
c\in\mathsf{C}
and
d\in\mathsf{D}
.

Let
\iota\colon\mathsf{D}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{C}
be an inclusion of small categories. By
[
33
, Proposition 5.1]
, the induced nerve
N\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D})
is fully faithful if and only if every object
c\in\mathsf{C}
is canonically the colimit of the forgetful functor
\mathsf{D}/c\to\mathsf{C}
,
(f\colon\iota(d)\to c)\mapsto\iota(d)
. In this case, the inclusion
\iota\colon\mathsf{D}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{C}
is called
dense
.

Example 2.7
.

The nerve of a small category
\mathsf{C}
is described by the image of
\mathsf{C}
under the nerve functor induced by the dense inclusion
\Delta\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Cat}
, where
\mathsf{Cat}
is the category of small categories, and
\Delta
is the
simplex category
of non-empty finite ordinals and order preserving morphisms.

2.3.
Weber’s abstract nerve theory

Every functor admits an (up to isomorphism) unique
bo-ff factorisation
as a bijective on objects functor followed by a fully faithful functor. For example, if
\mathbb{M}=(M,\mu,\eta)
is a monad on a category
\mathsf{C}
, and
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
is the EM category of algebras for
\mathbb{M}
, then the free functor
\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
has bo-ff factorisation
\mathsf{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{\mathbb{M}}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
, where
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbb{M}}
is the Kleisli category whose objects are the
free
\mathbb{M}
-algebras
of the form
(Mc,\mu^{\mathbb{M}}(c))
for
c\in\mathsf{C}
. See e.g.
[
35
, Section VI.5]
.

Let
\mathsf{D}\subset\mathsf{C}
be a dense subcategory and
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
the full subcategory of
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbb{M}}
obtained in the bo-ff factorisation of the functor
\mathsf{D}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{C}\xrightarrow{\text{ free}}\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
.
Consider the following diagram:

(2.8)

\textstyle{\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
forget
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
b.o.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathsf{D}}}
free
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D}).}

where
j^{*}
is the pullback of the bijective on objects functor
j\colon\mathsf{D}\to\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
. The left square of (
2.8
) commutes by definition, and the right square commutes up to natural isomorphism (c.f.,
[
6
, Section 1.6]
).

If the canonical colimit cocones
\mathsf{D}/c
lift to colimit cocones in
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
, then
\mathsf{D}
is said to
provide arities for
\mathbb{M}
. In this case, the fully faithful functor
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\to\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
is also dense and so the induced nerve
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})
is fully faithful. Moreover, by
[
52
, Section 4]
, the image of
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
in
\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})
consists of precisely those objects that are mapped, up to equivalence, by
j^{*}
to the image of
N_{\mathsf{D}}
in
\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D})
. This is the
Segal condition
for the nerve theorem.

More generally, if
\mathsf{D}\subset\mathsf{C}
is a dense subcategory and
\mathbb{M}
is a monad on
\mathsf{C}
such that the induced nerve
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
in (
2.8
) is fully faithful and the Segal condition is satisfied, then
\mathbb{M}
is said to be
\mathsf{D}
-nervous

[
7
]
. Having arities is a sufficient, but not necessary condition for a monad to be nervous. Indeed, neither the modular operad monad of
[
41
]
nor the circuit algebra monad constructed in this work (
Section

7.2
) have arities, but they are both nervous (c.f.,
Section

8
and
[
41
, Section 8]
).

Remark 2.9
.

In many cases of interest,
\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{psh}(\mathcal{E})
is the category of presheaves on some category
\mathcal{E}
. As the Yoneda embedding
\mathcal{E}\to\mathsf{C}
is fully faithful and dense, any full subcategory
\mathsf{D}\subset\mathsf{C}
that contains the representables is also dense in
\mathsf{C}
(and
\mathcal{E}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{D}
embeds as a full dense subcategory) so that the induced nerve
\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D})
is fully faithful. If
\mathbb{M}
is a
\mathsf{D}
-nervous monad on
\mathsf{C}
, then the Segal condition has a particularly nice form: A presheaf
P
on
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
is equivalent to the nerve of an
\mathbb{M}
-algebra if and only if, for all
d\in\mathsf{D}
,

(2.10)

P(jd)\cong\mathrm{lim}_{(x,f)\in\mathcal{E}/d}j^{*}P(x).

Bourke and Garner
[
7
]
call this situation
the presheaf context
.

Both the original Segal condition for the simplicial nerve theorem for small categories, and a Segal condition for the dendroidal nerve of an operad, may be obtained from monads with arities in the presheaf context (see e.g.,
[
52
, Section 4]
or
[
27
, Section 2]
for details). The presheaf context also applies for the nerve theorems for modular operads
[
41
, Theorem 8.2]
and circuit algebras (
Theorem

8.4
), though the corresponding monads on
\mathsf{GS}
do not have arities in these cases.

Remark 2.11
.

(See also Remarks
2.9
,
8.18
and
8.17
.) A special class of monad with arities is described by
strongly cartesian monads

[
6
]
(called
parametric right adjoint
monads in
[
52
]
and
polynomial monads
in
[
11
]
). These are
cartesian
monads (they satisfy certain pullback conditions) that are
locally right adjoint
(certain functors of slice categories are right adjoints). For a strongly cartesian monad
\mathbb{M}
on
\mathsf{C}
, there is a canonical choice of arities
\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{M}}\subset\mathsf{C}
(see e.g.,
[
6
, section 2]
). Strongly cartesian monads on presheaf categories –with their associated Segal condition
2.10
– are central to the theory of
algebraic patterns
developed in
[
11
]
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a strongly cartesian monad on
\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{psh}(\mathcal{E})
and let
\mathsf{D}\subset\mathsf{C}
provide canonical arities for
\mathbb{M}
. Consider the diagram (
2.8
) for
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathsf{D}
. In this case,
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
is dense in
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
, the induced nerve is fully faithful, and
P\in\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})
is, up to equivalence, in the image of the nerve
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
if and only if
P
satisfies (
2.10
). Since
\mathbb{M}
is strongly cartesian, there is, by
[
52
, Proposition 2.6]
, a wide subcategory
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{ref}}\subset\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
of so-called
generic morphisms
or
refinements

[
27
]
in
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
such that every morphism in
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
factors as a refinement followed by a morphism in the image of
\mathsf{D}
.

The strongly cartesian monad
\mathbb{M}
on
\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{psh}(\mathcal{E})
then has the form

(2.12)

M(P)(x)\cong\mathrm{colim}_{(y,f)\in x/\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{ref}}}\mathrm{lim}_{(e,\alpha)\in\mathcal{E}/_{\mathsf{D}}y}P(e),\qquad\text{ for all }P\in\mathsf{C},x\in\mathcal{E}.

In the terminology of
[
11
]
, the factorisation system on
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
, together with the underlying category
\mathcal{E}
, then describes an
extendable algebraic pattern
with corresponding
free Segal
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
-space monad

\mathbb{M}
. (See Remarks
2.20
and
8.18
for an outline of how this applies to the nerve theorems for circuit algebras and modular operads.)

2.4.
Applying distributive laws to prove nerve theorems

The proof of the nerve theorem
Theorem

8.4
for circuit algebras, like the proof of the modular operad nerve theorem of
[
41
]
, exploits the distributive law decompositions of the corresponding composite monad.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a monad on an essentially small category
\mathsf{C}
with subcategory
\mathsf{D}\subset\mathsf{C}
, and let
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\subset\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
be the subcategory obtained in the bo-ff factorisation of
\mathsf{D}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
.
Even if
\mathsf{D}
itself does not provide arities for
\mathbb{M}
, if
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1}
is a composite monad, then the decomposition of
\mathbb{M}
may provide other opportunities to apply the results of
[
6
]
in order to prove that the induced nerve
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})
is fully faithful:

Example 2.13
.

In the simplest case, let
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{2}\mathbb{M}_{1}
be a composite monad on
\mathsf{C}
induced by a distributive law
\lambda\colon M_{1}M_{2}\Rightarrow M_{2}M_{1}
, and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
be the lift of
\mathbb{M}_{2}
to
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{1}}
. Let
\mathsf{D}\subset\mathsf{C}
be a full subcategory,
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
be the category obtained in the bo-ff factorisation of the functor
\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
and let
\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\subset\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{1}}
be the full subcategory such that the bijective on objects functor
j\colon\mathsf{D}\to\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
factors through
\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}
. This is illustrated in Diagram (
2.14
), with
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}_{1}
and the free and forgetful functors
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}=\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
corresponding to the monadic adjunction associated to the lift
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
on
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{1}}
. The functor
\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\to\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
induced by the lifted monad
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
also has bo-ff factorisation through
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
. So, if
\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}
provides arities for
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
, then the nerve
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
is fully faithful.

(2.14)

\textstyle{\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
forget
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
b.o.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
forget
free
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime},\mathsf{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
b.o.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathsf{D}}}
free
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D}).}

More generally, let
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1}
be a composite monad on
\mathsf{C}
and let
\mathsf{D}\subset\mathsf{C}
be a full subcategory. For each
1\leq i<n
, the monads
\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{i+1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{i}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1}
on
\mathsf{C}
are composable. In particular,
\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{i+1}
lifts to a monad
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{(i+1)}
on the EM category
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{i}\dots\mathbb{M}_{i+1}}
.

There is a factorisation of the diagram (
2.8
) as

(2.15)

\textstyle{\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Theta_{\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
forget
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{n}^{*}}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{n}}
b.o.
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
free
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Theta_{\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
forget
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{1}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\scriptstyle{j_{1}}
b.o.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathsf{D}}}
free
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D}).}

The induced nerve
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
is fully faithful if, for some
1\leq i<n
, the lift of
\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{(i+1)}
to
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{i}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1}}
has arities
\Theta_{\mathbb{M}_{i}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}}
(as illustrated in (
2.15
)).

Example 2.16
.

(See also
Example

2.4
.) Once again, let
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{2}\mathbb{M}_{1}
be a composite monad on
\mathsf{C}
induced by a distributive law
\lambda\colon M_{1}M_{2}\Rightarrow M_{2}M_{1}
. If the monad
\mathbb{M}_{1}
extends to a monad
{\mathbb{M}_{1}}_{*}
on
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}}
such that
(\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}})^{{\mathbb{M}_{1}}_{*}}\cong\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}\mathbb{M}_{1}}
, then, by letting
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}_{2}
in Diagram (
2.14
), the same technique as in
Example

2.13
may be applied.

For example, in
[
41
, Theorem 7.46]
, the modular operad monad on
\mathsf{GS}
is obtained, via a distributive law, as a composite
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
. The proof of the nerve theorem and Segal condition (
[
41
, Theorem 8.2]
) follows from the fact that the monad
\mathbb{T}
extends to a monad
\mathbb{T}_{*}
, on the category
\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}
of
\mathbb{D}
-algebras, whose EM category of algebras coincides with the EM category of algebras for
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
on
\mathsf{GS}
. Whilst
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
does not have arities,
\mathbb{T}_{*}
does.

In
Section

8
, the proof of
Theorem

8.4
extends the proof of the modular operadic nerve theorem (
Example

2.16
) but requires a composite
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
, obtained via iterated distributive laws, of three monads on
\mathsf{GS}
, and uses both lifts and extensions of monads. The general case is outlined in the following example.

Example 2.17
.

Examples
2.13
and
2.16
may be combined: Let
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{3}\mathbb{M}_{2}\mathbb{M}_{1}
be a monad on
\mathsf{C}
and let
\mathsf{D}\subset\mathsf{C}
be a full and dense subcategory. Assume that
\mathbb{M}_{1}
extends to a monad
\mathbb{M}_{1*}
on the EM category
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}}
of algebras for
\mathbb{M}_{2}
and that
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}\mathbb{M}_{1}}\cong(\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}})^{\mathbb{M}_{1*}}
. By the theory of iterated distributive laws
[
10
]
,
\mathbb{M}_{3}
lifts functorially to both
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{1}}
and
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}\mathbb{M}_{1}}
. Hence, since
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}\mathbb{M}_{1}}\cong(\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}})^{\mathbb{M}_{1*}}
, the distributive law
M_{1}M_{3}\Rightarrow M_{3}M_{1}
between the monads
\mathbb{M}_{1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{3}
on
\mathsf{C}
lifts to a distributive law between the extension
{\mathbb{M}_{1*}}
of
\mathbb{M}_{1}
, and the lift
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{3}
of
\mathbb{M}_{3}
to
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}}
. Moreover, algebras for
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{3}{\mathbb{M}_{1*}}
on
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}}
correspond to algebras for
\mathbb{M}
on
\mathsf{C}
.

It follows that the nerve induced by the bo-ff factorisation
\mathsf{D}\to\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\to\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
is fully faithful if
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}_{3}{\mathbb{M}_{1*}}
has arities obtained from the full subcategory
\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{M}_{2}}\subset\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}}
with the same objects as
\mathsf{D}
(as in Diagram (
2.14
) with
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}_{2}
).

Remark 2.18
.

It is important to note that the conditions in each of the Examples
2.13
-
2.17
are not sufficient to establish a Segal condition in terms of presheaves on the category
\mathsf{D}
. The Segal condition in
[
41
, Theorem 8.2]
and
Theorem

8.4
, follows from the observation that the embedding
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
(described in
Section

6.3
) is dense.

Following Remarks
2.9
and
2.11
, I conclude this section with remarks on related results, specific to the presheaf context, that can be used to obtain additional proofs of the nerve theorem
8.4
. This is further explained in Remarks
8.18
and
8.17
.

Remark 2.19
.

Let
k\colon\mathcal{E}_{1}\to\mathcal{E}_{2}
be a bijective on objects functor and, for
i=1,2
, let
\mathbb{M}_{i}
be a monad on
\mathsf{C}_{i}=\mathsf{psh}(\mathcal{E}_{i})
such that the pullback
k^{*}\colon\mathsf{C}_{2}\to\mathsf{C}_{1}
describes a monad morphism
(\mathsf{C}_{1},\mathbb{M}_{1})\to(\mathsf{C}_{1},\mathbb{M}_{1})
by
k^{*}\mathbb{M}_{2}=\mathbb{M}_{1}k^{*}
. By
[
6
, Proposition 4.4]
, if
\mathbb{M}_{1}
is strongly cartesian, then so is
\mathbb{M}_{2}
and the canonical arities for
\mathbb{M}_{2}
may be obtained from those of
\mathbb{M}_{2}
by extension along
k
.

In the case of the circuit algebra and modular operad monads
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
on
\mathsf{GS}
, the forgetful functor
\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}\to\mathsf{GS}
associated to the monad
\mathbb{D}
on
\mathsf{GS}
is the pullback of the bijective on objects inclusion
k\colon{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}\hookrightarrow{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}
described in
Section

6.2
. The corresponding nerve theorems then follow, as in
Remark

8.17
, by showing that the monads
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{T}
are strongly cartesian on
\mathsf{GS}
and admit extension, along
k
, to
\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}
.

Remark 2.20
.

Recall Remarks
2.9
and
2.11
. Let
\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{psh}(\mathcal{E})
and
\mathsf{D}
be a full subcategory containing the representables. Further, let
\mathbb{M}
be a
\mathsf{D}
-nervous monad on
\mathsf{C}
. So, the nerve
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})
in Diagram (
2.8
) is fully faithful and presheaves in its essential image satisfy the presheaf Segal condition (
2.10
). Assume also that
\mathsf{D}
is the smallest full subcategory of
\mathsf{C}
with respect to which
\mathbb{M}
is nervous.

Now, let
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
admit an algebraic pattern structure – with elementary objects in
\mathcal{E}
– as in
[
11
]
. This means that there is a factorisation system
(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{ref}},\mathsf{D}^{\prime})
on
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
such that presheaves in the essential image of
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
must also satisfy a second Segal condition

(2.21)

P(j^{\prime}d)\cong\mathrm{lim}_{(x,f^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}/d}{j^{\prime}}^{*}P(x),

where
j^{\prime}\colon\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
is the bijective on objects inclusion and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subset\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
is the full subcategory on the objects from
\mathcal{E}
.
Moreover, if the factorisation of
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
describes an extendable algebraic pattern as in
[
11
, Proposition 8.8]
, then there is a strongly cartesian monad
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
on
\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{psh}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
, given by

M^{\prime}(P)(x)\cong\mathrm{colim}_{(y,f)\in x/\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}^{\mathrm{ref}}}\mathrm{lim}_{(e,\alpha^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}/y}P(e),\qquad\text{ for all }P\in\mathsf{C},x\in\mathcal{E},

whose EM category of algebras is precisely
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
.

This is summarised in the following diagram, where – as before (
2.8
) – the left squares commute and the right squares commute up to natural isomorphism:

(2.22)

\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
dense
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
dense
\scriptstyle{j^{\prime}}
b.o.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathsf{D}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\text{free}^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D}^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\text{forget}^{\mathbb{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\text{forget}^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{{j^{\prime}}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
dense
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
dense
\scriptstyle{j}
b.o.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathsf{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\text{free}^{\mathbb{M}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D}).}

For example, the situation of
Remark

2.19
is obtained if there is a functor
i\colon\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
such that
j^{\prime}\circ i=j
, and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is the extension – along the restricted pullback
i_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}\colon\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\to\mathsf{C}
- of a strongly cartesian monad
\mathbb{M}_{0}
on
\mathsf{C}
.

It is natural to ask under what conditions such a functor
i\colon\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
exists, and, furthermore whether it is possible to classify the nervous monads
\mathbb{M}
for which (as with the circuit algebra and modular operad nerve constructions of
Theorem

8.4
and
[
41
, Theorem 8.2]
) there exists an iterated distributive law describing
\mathbb{M}
from which the strongly cartesian monad
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
on
\mathsf{C}^{\prime}
may be obtained as an extension (and/or lift) as in Examples
2.13
-
2.17
.

3.
Graphical species, modular operads, and circuit algebras

Given a set
\mathfrak{D}
, let
\small{\mathsf{list}}(\mathfrak{D})=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{D}^{n}
be the set of finite ordered sets
\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
of elements of
\mathfrak{D}
(so
\small{\mathsf{list}}(\mathfrak{D})
is the set underlying the free associative monoid on
\mathfrak{D}
).
An
(involutive) palette
is a pair
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
of a set
\mathfrak{C}
with involution
\omega\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}
(see also
[
41
,
42
]
).

Let
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
be a palette. In
[
42
]
,
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured circuit algebras and modular operads
enriched
in a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathsf{X},\otimes,I)
were characterised axiomatically, as symmetric
\small{\mathsf{list}}(\mathfrak{C})
-graded monoids in
\mathsf{X}
equipped with a contraction operation satisfying certain axioms (see
[
42
, Proposition 5.4]
and
[
42
, Definition 5.7]
).

This section considers structures that satisfy the same axioms, but now defined
internally
to a category
\mathsf{V}
with sufficient (co)limits. Simply put, this means that the “grading” is now by some involutive object of
\mathsf{X}
. When
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{Set}
, the enriched and internal definitions coincide.

3.1.
Symmetric species

Let
\mathsf{Set_{f}}
denote the category of finite sets and functions and let
{\mathbf{B}}\subset\mathsf{Set_{f}}
be the groupoid of finite sets and bijections.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{n}
denote the set
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
(so
\mathbf{0}=\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
), and let
\Sigma_{n}
denote the group of permutations on
\mathbf{n}
. Let
\Sigma=\bigoplus_{n}\Sigma_{n}
be the
symmetric groupoid
(with
\Sigma(n,n)=\Sigma_{n}
).

As
\Sigma
is a skeleton for
{\mathbf{B}}
, for any category
\mathsf{V}
, a
\mathsf{V}
-valued presheaf
P\colon{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{V}
, also called a
(monochrome
or
single-sorted) species
[
28
]
in
\mathsf{V}
, determines a presheaf
\Sigma^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{V}
by restriction.
And conversely, a
\Sigma
-presheaf
Q
may always be extended to a
{\mathbf{B}}
-presheaf
Q_{{\mathbf{B}}}
, by setting

(3.1)

Q_{{\mathbf{B}}}(X)\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathbf{n},f)\in\Sigma/X}Q(n)\ \text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

Hence, a species
P\colon{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{V}
is equivalently a sequence
(P(n))_{n}
of objects of
\mathsf{V}
such that
\Sigma_{n}
acts on
P(n)
for all
n
.

For a set
\mathfrak{D}
, the associative (concatenation) product
\bm{c}\bm{d}=\bm{c}\oplus\bm{d}
on
\small{\mathsf{list}}(\mathfrak{D})
is given by

\bm{c}\bm{d}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(c_{1},\dots,c_{m},d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\text{ for }\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{m}),\bm{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\text{ in }\small{\mathsf{list}}(\mathfrak{D}).

The empty list is the unit for
\oplus
and is denoted by
\varnothing
(or
\varnothing_{\mathfrak{D}}
).

The symmetric groupoid
\Sigma
acts on
\small{\mathsf{list}}(\mathfrak{D})
from the right by
\sigma\colon(\bm{c}\sigma)\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(c_{\sigma 1},\dots,c_{\sigma n})\mapsto\bm{c}
, for all
\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}
. The monoidal groupoid so obtained is the
free symmetric groupoid on
\mathfrak{D}
denoted by
\Sigma^{\mathfrak{D}}
. In particular, if
\mathfrak{D}=\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
, then
\Sigma^{\mathfrak{D}}=\{\varnothing\}
.

An
\mathfrak{D}
-coloured species in
\mathsf{V}
is a
\mathsf{V}
-valued presheaf on
\Sigma^{\mathfrak{D}}
. This is equivalently described by a collection
(P(\bm{c}))_{\bm{c}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}(\mathfrak{D})}
of objects of
\mathsf{V}
such that
\Sigma_{n}
(right) acts on
P(n)=\coprod_{\bm{c}\in\mathfrak{D}^{n}}P^{\prime}(\bm{c})
for all
n
.

3.2.
Graphical species

Graphical species in
\mathsf{Set}
, introduced in
[
27
]
to describe various generalisations of (coloured) operads, generalise the notion of coloured species from
Section

3.1
and have been used to describe coloured modular operads (called
compact closed categories
in
[
27
]
) in
[
27
,
41
,
23
,
24
]
. For more details on graphical species in
\mathsf{Set}
, particularly regarding the involutive palettes
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
that encode their expressive power, the reader is referred to
[
41
, Section 1]
.

This section provides a short discussion on graphical species taking values in an arbitrary category
\mathsf{V}
. For convenience,
\mathsf{V}
will always be assumed to have sufficient limits and colimits. The terminal object of
\mathsf{V}
is denoted by
*
and the initial object is denoted by
0=0_{\mathsf{V}}
.

As before, denote the groupoid of finite sets and bijections by
{\mathbf{B}}
. The category
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
(called
\bm{elGr}
in
[
27
]
) is obtained from
{\mathbf{B}}
by adjoining a distinguished object
\lx@sectionsign
that satisfies

•

{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\lx@sectionsign,\lx@sectionsign)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
with generator
\tau
;

•

for each finite set
X
and each element
x\in X
, there is a morphism
ch_{x}\in{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\lx@sectionsign,X)
that “chooses”
x
, and
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\lx@sectionsign,X)=\{ch_{x}\ ,\ ch_{x}\circ\tau\}_{x\in X}\cong X\amalg X
;

•

for all finite sets
X
and
Y
,
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(X,Y)={\mathbf{B}}(X,Y)
;

•

morphisms are equivariant with respect to the action of
{\mathbf{B}}
. That is,
ch_{f(x)}=f\circ ch_{x}\in{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\lx@sectionsign,Y)
for all
x\in X
and all bijections
f\colon X\xrightarrow{\cong}Y
.

Definition 3.2
.

A
graphical species
S
in a category
\mathsf{V}
is a presheaf
S\colon{{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{V}
. The category of graphical species in
\mathsf{V}
is denoted by
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{psh_{\mathsf{V}}}({{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}})
. When
\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{Set}
, we write
\mathsf{GS}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{Set}}
.

So, a graphical species
S
is described by a
\mathsf{V}
-valued species
(S_{X})_{X\in{\mathbf{B}}}
, and an object
S_{\lx@sectionsign}\in\mathsf{V}
with involution
S_{\tau}\in\mathsf{V}(S_{\lx@sectionsign},S_{\lx@sectionsign})
,
together with a family of projections
S(ch_{x})\colon S_{X}\to S_{\lx@sectionsign}
, defined for all finite sets
X
, and all
x\in X
, and equivariant with respect to the actions of
{\mathbf{B}}
and
\tau
. In the definitions of circuit algebras and modular operads (Sections
3.3
&
3.4
), the objects
S_{X}
describe the (modular operad) operations, or morphisms, whereas the object
S_{\lx@sectionsign}
can be thought of as describing the “colours”, or generating the “object object”.

Example 3.3
.

The terminal graphical species
{K^{\mathsf{V}}}
is the constant (monochrome) graphical species that sends
\lx@sectionsign
and all finite sets
X
to the terminal object
*
in
\mathsf{V}
.

Definition 3.4
.

A morphism
\gamma\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}(S,S^{\prime})
is
colour-
or
palette-preserving
if its component at
\lx@sectionsign
is the identity on
({S_{\lx@sectionsign}},S_{\tau})
. A graphical species
S\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
is called monochrome if
S_{\lx@sectionsign}=*
.

If
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
is an involutive palette in
\mathsf{Set}
, objects of the subcategory
{\mathsf{GS}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}}\subset\mathsf{GS}
of
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured graphical species in
\mathsf{Set}
are graphical species
S\in\mathsf{GS}
such that
(S_{\lx@sectionsign},S_{\tau})=(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
.
Morphisms are palette-preserving morphisms.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
S\in{\mathsf{GS}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}}
. For a finite set
X
,
S_{X}=\coprod_{\bm{c}\in\mathfrak{C}^{X}}S_{\bm{c}}
, where, for each
\bm{c}\in\mathfrak{C}^{X}
, the set
S_{\bm{c}}
is the
\bm{c}
-(coloured) arity of
S
defined as the fibre above
\bm{c}
of the map
(S(ch_{x}))_{x\in X}\colon{S_{X}}\to\mathfrak{C}^{X}
.

It follows that
S
defines a
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured species
S^{\prime}\colon(\Sigma^{\mathfrak{C}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
by restriction. Conversely, for any involutive palette
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
, a
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured species
P\colon(\Sigma^{\mathfrak{C}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
extends to a graphical species
\hat{P}\in{\mathsf{GS}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}}
by (
3.1
).

Example 3.6
.

For any palette
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
, the
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured graphical species
{{K}}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}
given by
{{K}}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}_{\bm{c}}=\{*\}
for all
\bm{c}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}(\mathfrak{C})
is terminal in
{\mathsf{GS}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}}
. If
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)=\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
, then
K^{\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}}_{\mathbf{0}}=\{*\}
and
K^{\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}}_{X}=\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
for
X\not\cong\mathbf{0}
.

The element category (
Definition

2.5
) of a
\mathsf{Set}
-valued graphical species
S\in\mathsf{GS}
is denoted by
\mathsf{el}{(S)}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{el}_{{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}}(S)
.

Example 3.7
.

Let
(\mathfrak{Di},\sigma_{\mathfrak{Di}})
(called
\{\wideparen{\uparrow,\ \downarrow}\}
in
[
42
]
) be the involutive palette in
\mathsf{Set}
described by the unique non-trivial involution
\sigma_{\mathfrak{Di}}
on the two-element set
\mathfrak{Di}=\{{\mathrm{in}},\ {\mathrm{out}}\}
. This was used in
[
42
, Section 3.2]
to describe (monochrome) oriented circuit algebras (monochrome wheeled props), and in
[
41
, Section 1.1]
to describe props and wheeled properads.

If
Di
is the terminal
(\mathfrak{Di},\sigma_{\mathfrak{Di}})
-coloured
graphical species in
\mathsf{Set}
, then, for all finite sets
X
,
Di_{X}=\mathfrak{Di}^{X}
is the set of partitions of
X=X_{{\mathrm{in}}}\amalg X_{{\mathrm{out}}}
into
input
and
output
sets and morphisms
Di_{X}\to Di_{Y}
are bijections
X\xrightarrow{\cong}Y
that preserve the partitions. Objects of the category
{\mathsf{GS}^{(\mathfrak{Di},\sigma_{\mathfrak{Di}})}}
are called
monochrome oriented graphical species
in
\mathsf{Set}
.

Since
({\mathrm{in}})\cong({\mathrm{out}})
in
\mathsf{el}{(Di)}
, the element category
\mathsf{el}{(Di)}
of
Di
is equivalent to the category
({\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathrm{op}})^{+}
obtained from
{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}
by adjoining a distinguished object
\downarrow
(the
directed exceptional edge
), and morphisms
i_{x},o_{y}\colon(X_{{\mathrm{in}}},X_{{\mathrm{out}}})\to(\downarrow)
defined for each
x\in X_{{\mathrm{in}}}
and
y\in X_{{\mathrm{out}}}
.

Definition 3.8
.

The category
{\mathsf{OGS}_{\mathsf{V}}}
of
oriented graphical species in
\mathsf{V}
is the presheaf category
\mathsf{psh_{\mathsf{V}}}(\mathsf{el}{(Di)})
.

For all
\mathsf{Set}
-valued graphical species
S^{\prime}\in\mathsf{GS}
, there is a canonical equivalence
\mathsf{GS}/S^{\prime}\cong\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{el}{(S^{\prime})})
such that
\gamma\in\mathsf{GS}(S,S^{\prime})
describes a presheaf
\tilde{S}\in\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{el}{(S^{\prime})})
by

\tilde{S}(\phi)=\gamma^{-1}(\phi)
for all
\phi\in\mathsf{el}{(S^{\prime})}
.

Example 3.9
.

(See also
[
41
, Example 1.10]
.)
Assume that
\mathsf{V}
has finite products and coproducts. Let
Di^{\mathsf{V}}\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
be the graphical species given by
{Di^{\mathsf{V}}}_{\lx@sectionsign}=*\amalg*
(and
{Di^{\mathsf{V}}}_{\tau}
acts by swapping the components), and, for each finite set
\mathcal{X}
,
{Di^{\mathsf{V}}}_{X}=\coprod_{(X_{{\mathrm{in}}},X_{{\mathrm{out}}})\in Di_{X}}(*)
.

There is a canonical equivalence of categories
{\mathsf{OGS}_{\mathsf{V}}}\simeq\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}/Di^{\mathsf{V}}
. If
(S,\gamma)\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}/Di^{\mathsf{V}}
, then the corresponding presheaf
\tilde{S}\in\mathsf{psh_{\mathsf{V}}}(\mathsf{el}{(Di)})={\mathsf{OGS}_{\mathsf{V}}}
is such that, for all
\mathcal{C}\in{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
and all
\phi\in S_{\mathcal{C}}
(so
\phi
is either a pair of finite sets or an element of
\{{\mathrm{in}},{\mathrm{out}}\}
),
\tilde{S}(\phi)
is given by the pullback

\textstyle{\tilde{S}(\phi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{\mathcal{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{\mathcal{C}}}
\textstyle{{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{inc_{\phi}}
\textstyle{Di^{\mathsf{V}}_{\mathcal{C}}*}

where
inc_{\phi}
is the obvious map that picks out the component indexed by
\phi
in
Di^{\mathsf{V}}_{\mathcal{C}}=\coprod_{Di_{\mathcal{C}}}*
.

Since
\mathsf{V}
is always assumed to have sufficient (co)limits, these two descriptions of oriented graphical species – as
\mathsf{V}
-valued presheaves on
\mathsf{el}{(Di)}
or as objects of
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
with morphisms to
Di^{\mathsf{V}}
– will be used interchangeably in what follows.

Remark 3.10
.

When
\mathsf{V}
has finite products and coproducts, addition of natural numbers in
\Sigma
induces a symmetric monoidal product on the category of species in
\mathsf{V}
by

(3.11)

(S\otimes T)_{X}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\coprod_{(Y,Z),Y\amalg Z=X}S_{Y}\times T_{Z},\text{ for all }X\in{\mathbf{B}}

for species
S,T
and all finite sets
X
. It is easily checked (see also
[
42
, Section 2.1]
) that monoids for this product correspond to (lax) monoidal presheaves on
\Sigma
.

This extends to a symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
(and also
{\mathsf{OGS}_{\mathsf{V}}}
) whereby, given
S,T\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
, the graphical species
S\otimes T\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
, has
(S\otimes T)_{X}=\coprod_{(Y,Z),Y\amalg Z=X}S_{Y}\times T_{Z}
as in (
3.11
) and
(S\otimes T)_{\lx@sectionsign}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}S_{\lx@sectionsign}\amalg T_{\lx@sectionsign}
.

By the universal property of the coproduct, the involutions on
S_{\lx@sectionsign}
and
T_{\lx@sectionsign}
induce an involution on
(S\otimes T)_{\lx@sectionsign}
and the
{\mathbf{B}}
-actions on
S
and
T
separately induce a
{\mathbf{B}}
-action on the collection
((S\otimes T)_{X})_{X\in{\mathbf{B}}}
.
For
x\in X
, the morphism
(S\otimes T)(ch^{X}_{x})\colon(S\otimes T)_{X}\to(S\otimes T)_{\lx@sectionsign}
is induced by the maps

(S\otimes T)(ch^{Y\amalg Z}_{x})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\iota_{S}\circ S(ch^{Y}_{x})\circ p_{S_{Y}}\colon S_{Y}\times T_{Z}\to S_{\lx@sectionsign}&\text{ when }x\in Y\\
\iota_{T}\circ T(ch^{Z}_{x})\circ p_{T_{Z}}\colon S_{Y}\times T_{Z}\to T_{\lx@sectionsign}&\text{ when }x\in Z.\end{array}\right.

Here, the maps
p_{(-)}
from
S_{Y}\times T_{Z}
are the projections and
\iota_{(-)}
are the maps to the coproduct
(S\otimes T)_{\lx@sectionsign}
.

It is straightforward to check that
S\otimes T\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
and that
\otimes
is associative and the graphical species
I
defined by
I_{\lx@sectionsign}=0
,
I_{\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}}=*
and
I_{X}=0
for
X\neq\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
(so
I=I_{\mathsf{V}}=K^{\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}}
when
\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{Set}
)
defines a unit for
\otimes
, making
(\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}},\otimes,I)
into a symmetric monoidal category.

3.3.
Modular operads

This section generalises the definition of modular operads in
\mathsf{Set}
from
[
41
, Section 1.3]
to define modular operads
internal to
arbitrary categories with finite limits. Modular operads internal to a category
\mathsf{V}
are defined as graphical species in
\mathsf{V}
equipped with a binary (unital) multiplication operation and unary contraction operation satisfying coherence axioms that say, informally, that the order of applying multiplications and contractions does not matter.

Let
S
be a graphical species in a category
\mathsf{V}
with finite limits. For finite sets
X
and
Y
with elements
x\in X
and
y\in Y
, let
(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x\ddagger y}\to S_{X}\times S_{Y}
denote the pullback

(3.12)

\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x\ddagger y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S(ch_{x})}
\textstyle{S_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S(ch_{y}\circ\tau)}
\textstyle{S_{\lx@sectionsign}.}

More generally, given distinct elements
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in X
and
y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\in Y
, let
(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}
be the limit of the collection
S_{X}\xrightarrow{S(ch_{x_{i}})}S_{\lx@sectionsign}\xleftarrow{S(ch_{y_{i}}\circ\tau)}S_{Y}
,
1\leq i\leq k
.

Let
\sigma_{\mathbf{2}}
denote the unique non-identity involution on
\mathbf{2}
. A morphism
\epsilon\in\mathsf{V}(S_{\lx@sectionsign},S_{\mathbf{2}})
is
unit-like for
S
if

(3.13)

\epsilon\circ\omega=S(\sigma_{\mathbf{2}})\circ\epsilon\colon S_{\lx@sectionsign}\to S_{\mathbf{2}},\text{ and }\ S(ch_{1})\circ\epsilon=id_{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}.

Definition 3.14
.

A
multiplication

\diamond
on
S
is a family of morphisms

\diamond_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y}\colon(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x\ddagger y}\to S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x,y\}},

in
\mathsf{V}
, defined for all pairs of finite sets
X
and
Y
, with elements
x\in X
and
y\in Y
, and such that

(1)

the obvious diagram

\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x\ddagger y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\quad S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x,y\}}}
\textstyle{(S_{Y}\times S_{X})^{y\ddagger x}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond_{Y,X}^{y\ddagger x}}

commutes in
\mathsf{V}
,

(2)

\diamond
is equivariant with respect to the
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
-action on
S
: if
\hat{\sigma}\colon X\setminus\{x\}\xrightarrow{\cong}W\setminus\{w\}
and
\hat{\rho}\colon Y\setminus\{y\}\xrightarrow{\cong}Z\setminus\{z\}
are restrictions of bijections
\sigma\colon X\xrightarrow{\cong}W
and
\rho\colon Y\xrightarrow{\cong}Z
such that
\sigma(x)=w
and
\rho(y)=z
, then

S(\hat{\sigma}\sqcup\hat{\rho})\ \diamond_{W,Z}^{w\ddagger z}=\diamond_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y}\ S(\sigma\sqcup\rho),

(where
\sigma\sqcup\rho\colon X\amalg Y\xrightarrow{\cong}W\amalg Z
is the block-wise permutation).

A multiplication
\diamond
on
S
is
unital
if
S
there is a unit-like morphism

\epsilon\in\mathsf{V}(S_{\lx@sectionsign},S_{\mathbf{2}})
such that,
for all finite sets
X
and all
x\in X
, the composite

(3.15)

\textstyle{S_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(id\times ch_{x})\circ\Delta}
\textstyle{S_{X}\times S_{\lx@sectionsign}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\times\epsilon}
\textstyle{S_{X}\times S_{\mathbf{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond_{X,\mathbf{2}}^{x\ddagger 2}}
\textstyle{S_{X}}

is the identity on
S_{X}
.

It is immediate from the definition (
3.13
) that any unit-like morphism in
\epsilon\in\mathsf{V}(S_{\lx@sectionsign},S_{\mathbf{2}})
is monomorphic and, by (
3.15
) and condition (1) of
Definition

3.14
, if a multiplication
\diamond
on
S
admits a unit
\epsilon
, then it is unique.

Now, let
S
be a graphical species in
\mathsf{V}
, and let
x\neq y
be distinct elements of a finite set
Z
. Let
(S_{Z})^{x\ddagger y}=S_{Z}^{y\ddagger x}
denote the equaliser

(3.16)

\textstyle{S_{Z}^{x\ddagger y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S(ch_{x})}
\scriptstyle{S(ch_{y}\circ\tau)}
\textstyle{S_{\lx@sectionsign}.}

More generally, for any morphism
f\colon C\to S_{Z}
in
\mathsf{V}
, let
C^{x\ddagger y}=C^{y\ddagger x}
denote the pullback of
f
along the universal map
S_{Z}^{x\ddagger y}\to S_{Z}
.
Given distinct elements
x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{k},y_{k}
of
S_{Z}
, and a morphism
f\colon C\to S_{Z}
in
\mathsf{V}
, then
C^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(C^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k-1}\ddagger y_{k-1}})^{x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}
is the obvious limit.

Invariance of
C^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}
under permutations
(x_{i},y_{i})\mapsto(x_{\sigma i},y_{\sigma i})
,
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
follows from invariance of the defining morphisms

\textstyle{C^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{Z}.}

Definition 3.17
.

A
(graphical species) contraction

\zeta
on
S
in
\mathsf{V}
is a family of maps
\zeta^{x\ddagger y}_{X}\colon S_{X}^{x\ddagger y}\to S_{X\setminus\{x,y\}}
defined for each finite set
X
and pair of distinct elements
x,y\in X
.

The contraction
\zeta
is equivariant with respect to the action of
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
on
S
: If
\hat{\sigma}\colon X\setminus\{x,y\}\xrightarrow{\cong}Z\setminus\{w,z\}
is the restriction of a bijection
\sigma\colon X\xrightarrow{\cong}Z
with
\sigma(x)=w
and
\sigma(y)=z
. Then

(3.18)

S(\hat{\sigma})\circ\zeta^{w\ddagger z}_{Z}=\zeta^{x\ddagger y}_{X}\circ S(\sigma)\colon S_{Z}^{w\ddagger z}\to S_{X\setminus\{x,y\}}.

In particular, by (
3.18
), if
\zeta
is a contraction on
S
, then
\zeta_{X}^{x\ddagger y}=\zeta_{X}^{y\ddagger x}
for all finite sets
X
and all pairs of distinct elements
x,y\in X
.

Definition 3.19
.

A nonunital modular operad
(S,\diamond,\zeta)
in
\mathsf{V}
is a graphical species
S\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
equipped with a multiplication
\diamond
and a contraction
\zeta
such that the following four coherence axioms are satisfied:

(M1)

Multiplication is associative
(see
Figure

1
(M1)):

For all finite set
X,Y,Z
and elements
x\in X
,
z\in Z
, and distinct
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
,

\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{Y}\times S_{Z})^{x\ddagger y_{1},y_{2}\ddagger z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y_{1}}\times id_{S_{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{id_{S_{X}}\times\diamond_{Y,Z}^{y_{2}\ddagger z}}
\textstyle{\left(S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x,y_{1}\}}\times S_{Z}\right)^{y_{2}\ddagger z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond^{y_{2}\ddagger z}_{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x,y_{1}\},Z}}
\textstyle{=}
\textstyle{\left(S_{X}\times S_{(Y\amalg Z)\setminus\{y_{2},z\}}\right)^{x\ddagger y_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond^{x\ddagger y_{1}}_{X,Y\amalg Z\setminus\{y_{2},z\}}}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y\amalg Z)\setminus\{x,y_{1},y_{2},z\}}.}

(M2)

Contractions commute
(see
Figure

1
(M2)):

For all finite sets
X
with distinct elements
w,x,y,z
, the following square commutes:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern
