(M3)

Multiplication and contraction commute
(see
Figure

1
(M2)):

For finite sets
X,Y
, distinct
x_{1},x_{2}
and
x_{3}
in
X
, and
y\in Y
, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{(S_{X}^{x_{1}\ddagger x_{2}}\times S_{Y})^{x_{3}\ddagger y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x_{1}\ddagger x_{2}}_{X}\times id_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\diamond^{x_{3}\ddagger y}_{X,Y}}
\textstyle{(S_{X\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\}}\times S_{Y)})^{x_{3}\ddagger y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond^{x_{3}\ddagger y}_{X\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\},Y}}
\textstyle{=}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x_{3},y\}}^{x_{1}\ddagger x_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x_{1}\ddagger x_{2}}_{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{3},y\}}}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},y\}}.}

(M4)

“Parallel multiplication” of pairs
(see
Figure

1
(M4)):

For finite sets
X
and
Y
, and distinct elements
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
, the following digram commutes:

\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\diamond{x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}_{X,Y}}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x_{1},y_{1}\}}^{x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}_{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{1},y_{1}\}}}
\textstyle{=}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x_{2},y_{2}\}}^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1}}{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{2},y_{2}\}}}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\}}.}

Figure 1
.
Modular operad axioms (M1)-(M4).

(Axioms (M1)-(M4) are illustrated in
Figure

1
.)

The category of nonunital, modular operads in
\mathsf{V}
is denoted by
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

A
(unital) modular operad
(S,\diamond,\zeta,\epsilon)
in
\mathsf{V}
is a graphical species
S\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
equipped with a unital multiplication
(\diamond,\epsilon)
and contraction
\zeta
such that
(S,\diamond,\zeta)\in{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}
. The category of modular operads in
\mathsf{V}
is denoted by
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

Remark 3.20
.

As discussed in
Section

3.2
,
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
has a monoidal category structure when
\mathsf{V}
admits finite coproducts. However, as will be explained in
Remark

6.29
, the categories
\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}
and
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}^{-}
are not closed under the monoidal product in
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

3.4.
Circuit algebras

A
circuit algebra internal to a category
\mathsf{V}
with finite limits (see
Definition

3.25
) is a graphical species
A
in
\mathsf{V}
that is equipped with an equivariant contraction and such that
(A(\mathbf{n}))_{\mathbf{n}}
describes a graded associative monoid. The monoid structure and contraction satisfy coherence axioms analogous to the (enriched) circuit algebra axioms
(c1)-(c3),(e1)
of

[
42
, Proposition 5.4]
.

Definition 3.21
.

A
(commutative) external product
on a graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{V}
is given by a
{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}
-indexed collection of equivariant morphisms
\boxtimes_{X,Y}:S_{X}\times S_{Y}\to S_{X\amalg Y},
in
\mathsf{V}
such that
\boxtimes_{Y,X}=\boxtimes_{X,Y}\circ\sigma_{Y,X}
(where
\sigma_{Y,X}\colon S_{Y}\times S_{X}\xrightarrow{\cong}S_{X}\times S_{Y}
is the swap isomorphism) and,
for all elements
x\in X
, the following diagram commutes in
\mathsf{V}
:

\textstyle{S_{X}\times S_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxtimes_{X,Y}}
\textstyle{S_{X\amalg Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S(ch_{x}^{X\amalg Y})}
\textstyle{S_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S(ch^{X}_{x})}
\textstyle{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}

An
external unit
for the external product
\boxtimes
is a distinguished morphism
0_{S}\colon*\to S_{\mathbf{0}}
such that, for all finite sets
X
, the composite

\textstyle{S_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{S_{X}\times*\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\times 0_{S}}
\textstyle{S_{X}\times S_{\mathbf{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxtimes}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg\mathbf{0})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{X}}

is the identity on
S_{X}
for all finite sets
X
.

Remark 3.22
.

Henceforth, the monoidal unit
0_{S}
for
\boxtimes
will be suppressed in the notation since most of the constructions in Sections
3
-
8
of this paper carry through without it.
To agree with the circuit algebra literature, external products in this paper are always assumed to have a unit. See also
Remark

5.17
, and
[
31
, Paragraph 4.7]
.

Recall (
Remark

3.10
) that, if
\mathsf{V}
has finite coproducts, then
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
is a symmetric monoidal category. It follows immediately from
Definition

3.21
that:

Lemma 3.23
.

A graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{V}
admits an external product
(\boxtimes,0_{S})
if and only if
(S,\boxtimes,0_{S})
is a monoid for the monoidal product
\otimes
on
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

An external product
\boxtimes
and contraction
\zeta
on a graphical species
S
induce an equivariant multiplication
\diamond
on
S
: for all finite sets
X
and
Y
, all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
, the multiplication
\diamond^{x\ddagger y}_{X,Y}
is defined by the composition

(3.24)

\diamond_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y}\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 32.3638pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-32.3638pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x\ddagger y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.42621pt\raise 6.59306pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.6153pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\boxtimes_{X,Y}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.3638pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 56.3638pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 86.3638pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(S_{X\amalg Y})^{x\ddagger y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 156.26563pt\raise 6.92557pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.56447pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x\ddagger y}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 193.89703pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x,y\}}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Definition 3.25
.

A
nonunital circuit algebra in
\mathsf{V}
is a graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{V}
, equipped with an external product
\boxtimes
and a contraction
\zeta
, such that the following three axioms (illustrated in
Figure

2
) are satisfied:

(C1)

External product is associative
(see
Figure

2
(C1)):

For all finite sets
X,Y,Z
,
the following square commutes:

\textstyle{S_{X}\times S_{Y}\times S_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxtimes_{X,Y}\ \times\ id_{S_{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{id_{S_{X}}\ \times\ \boxtimes_{Y,Z}}
\textstyle{S_{X\amalg Y}\times S_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxtimes_{(X\amalg Y),Z}}
\textstyle{=}
\textstyle{S_{X}\times S_{Y\amalg Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxtimes_{X,(Y\amalg Z)}}
\textstyle{S_{X\amalg Y\amalg Z}.}

(C2)

Order of contraction does not matter.
This corresponds with
Definition

3.19
(M2) (see also
Figure

1
(M2)).

(C3)

Contraction and external product commute
(see
Figure

2
(C1)):

For all finite sets
X
and
Y
, and distinct elements
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
, the following square commutes:

\textstyle{S_{X}^{x_{1}\ddagger x_{2}}\times S_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxtimes_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x_{1}\ddagger x_{2}}_{X}\ \times\ id_{S_{Y}}}
\textstyle{S_{X\amalg Y}^{x_{1}\ddagger x_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x_{1}\ddagger x_{2}}_{X\amalg Y}}
\textstyle{=}
\textstyle{S_{X\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\}}\times S_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxtimes_{X\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\},Y}}
\textstyle{S_{X\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\amalg Y}.}

Figure 2
.
Circuit algebra axioms (C1) and (C3). For axiom (C2), see
Figure

1
(M2).

Let
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}
denote the category of nonunital circuit algebras in
\mathsf{V}
and morphisms of graphical species that preserve the external product and contraction.

A
unital circuit algebra (in
\mathsf{V}
)
is a nonunital circuit algebra
(S,\boxtimes,\zeta)
together with a unit-like morphism
\epsilon\colon S_{\lx@sectionsign}\to S_{\mathbf{2}}
(
Definition

3.14
), such that, for all finite sets
X
and
x\in X
, the composite

(3.26)

\textstyle{S_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(id\times ch_{x})\circ\Delta}
\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{\lx@sectionsign})^{x\ddagger 2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\times\epsilon}
\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{\mathbf{2}})^{x\ddagger 2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxtimes}
\textstyle{{(S_{X\amalg\mathbf{2}})^{x\ddagger 2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x\ddagger 2}_{X\amalg\mathbf{2}}}
\textstyle{S_{X}}

is the identity on
S_{X}
. (Here
\Delta\colon S_{X}\to S_{X}\times S_{X}
is the diagonal and the last map makes use of the canonical isomorphism
(X\amalg\{1\})\setminus\{x\}\cong X
.)

Morphisms in the category
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}_{\mathsf{V}}
of circuit algebras in
\mathsf{V}
are morphisms in
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}
that preserve units.

For
\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{Set}
, we will write
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}_{\mathsf{V}}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}_{\mathsf{V}}
. Let
\bm{\mathsf{CA}}
be the category, defined in
[
42
, Section 5.2]
, of circuit algebras (of all colours) enriched in
\mathsf{Set}
. The following proposition follows immediately from
[
42
, Proposition 5.4]
and by unpacking
Definition

3.25
:

Proposition 3.27
.

The inclusion
\Sigma\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{B}}
induces a canonical equivalence of categories
\bm{\mathsf{CA}}\simeq{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}
.

Remark 3.28
.

Henceforth, unless explicitly stated otherwise, circuit algebras and modular operads will be assumed to be objects of the categories
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}_{\mathsf{V}}
and
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}_{\mathsf{V}}
(and
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}
,
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}
) internal to a category
\mathsf{V}
with sufficient limits, and we will not consider enriched circuit algebras.

By (
3.24
), a (nonunital) circuit algebra
(S,\boxtimes,\zeta)
admits a (nonunital) multiplication
\diamond_{S}
induced by
\boxtimes
and
\zeta
. It follows immediately from (
3.24
) and axioms (C1)-(C3), that
(S,\diamond_{S},\zeta)
satisfy (M1)-(M4). Hence, circuit algebras in
\mathsf{V}
canonically admit a modular operad structure.
This will be used in Sections
4
-
7.2
, to construct the composite monad for circuit algebras by modifying the modular operad monad of

[
41
]
.

In fact, we have the following proposition, analogous to
[
42
, Proposition 5.11]
, that will follow from
Theorem

7.9
,
Proposition

5.14
and
Lemma

7.4
, together with the classical theory of distributive laws
[
5
]
:

Proposition 3.29
.

There are canonical monadic adjunctions

(3.30)

\textstyle{{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptsize{\top}}
\textstyle{{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}_{\mathsf{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdash}
\textstyle{\vdash}
\textstyle{{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptsize{\top}}
\textstyle{{{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}_{\mathsf{V}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

between the categories of (nonunital) modular operads and (nonunital) circuit algebras in
\mathsf{V}
.

Proof.

By
Theorem

7.9
,
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}_{\mathsf{V}}
is the EM category of algebras
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}}
for the composite monad
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
on
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
, constructed using iterated distributive laws. Now,
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}=\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathbb{T}}
by
Proposition

6.2
,
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}_{\mathsf{V}}=\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}}
by
Theorem

6.27
(see also
[
41
]
), and
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}=\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{T}}
by
Proposition

5.14
and
Lemma

7.4
. Hence, the vertical adjunctions exist by the classical theory of distributive laws
[
5
]
.

The horizontal adjunctions – where the left adjoints correspond to freely adjoining modular operadic units – exist by
Proposition

6.25
.
∎

The right adjoints of the pairs
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}_{\mathsf{V}}\rightleftarrows{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}_{\mathsf{V}}
(and
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}\rightleftarrows{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}
) in
Proposition

3.29
correspond to the forgetful functor
(S,\boxtimes,\zeta)\mapsto(S,\diamond,\zeta)
where the multiplication
\diamond
is defined by suitable compositions
\zeta\circ\boxtimes
as in (
3.24
). The left adjoint is induced by the free graded monoid monad:

Let
\mathrm{Core}(X/\mathsf{Set_{f}})
be the maximal subgroupoid of the slice category
X/\mathsf{Set_{f}}
whose objects are morphisms of finite sets
f\colon X\to Y
(that may be thought of as
Y
-indexed partitions of
X
), and whose morphisms
(Y,f)\to(Y^{\prime},f^{\prime})
are isomorphisms
g\cong Y\to Y^{\prime}
such that
gf=f^{\prime}
. If
S
is a graphical species, then there is a graphical species
LS
defined by

LS_{X}=\mathrm{colim}_{(Y,f)\in\mathrm{Core}(X/\mathsf{Set_{f}})}\coprod_{y\in Y}S_{f^{-1}(y)}.

The free monoid structure induces the external product on
LS
, and, if
(S,\diamond,\zeta)
is a (nonunital) modular operad, then the modular operad axioms imply that the pair of operations
\zeta
and
\diamond
define a contraction on
LS
(see
Section

7.2
).

In particular, since units for the modular operadic multiplication are unique:

Corollary 3.31
.

The canonical forgetful functors
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}_{\mathsf{V}}\to{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}
and
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}_{\mathsf{V}}\to{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}
are inclusions of categories.

Example 3.32
.

By
[
13
,
42
]
, oriented circuit algebras enriched in a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathsf{X},\otimes,I)
are equivalent to (coloured) wheeled props enriched in
\mathsf{X}
.
The image of a (nonunital) wheeled prop under the forgetful functor
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}\to\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}
is its underlying (nonunital)
wheeled properad

[
22
,
53
]
. (See also
Example

3.6
,
Example

6.28
and
Corollary

8.6
and Proposition 3.26 and Example 5.10 of
[
42
]
.)

The graphical species
Di^{\mathsf{V}}
(Examples
3.7
&
3.9
) trivially admits a circuit algebra structure. Hence,
Example

3.32
motivates the following definition:

Definition 3.33
.

The category
{\bm{\mathfrak{WP}}}_{\mathsf{V}}
of
wheeled props in
\mathsf{V}
is defined as the slice category
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}_{\mathsf{V}}/Di^{\mathsf{V}}
. When
\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{Set}
, we write
{\bm{\mathfrak{WP}}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}{\bm{\mathfrak{WP}}}_{\mathsf{Set}}
.

The category of
nonunital wheeled props in
\mathsf{V}
is defined similarly by
{\bm{\mathfrak{WP}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}/Di^{\mathsf{V}}
.

Remark 3.34
.

By
Proposition

3.29
, a circuit algebra is a modular operad equipped with an external product that is compatible with the modular operadic multiplication in the sense of (
3.24
). By
Lemma

3.23
, a graphical species with external product
(S,\boxtimes,0_{S})
is just a monoid object in
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
so it is natural to ask whether there is a monoidal structure on
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}_{\mathsf{V}}
such that circuit algebras are precisely the monoid objects in
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}_{\mathsf{V}}
. This is not the case since, as will be explained in
Remark

6.29
,
{\bm{\mathfrak{MO}}}_{\mathsf{V}}
is not closed under the monoidal product on
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

4.
Background on graphs

The combinatorics of modular operads have been described in
[
41
, Section 8]
in terms of a category
\Xi
of connected graphs. The remainder of the present paper is concerned with an analogous construction and graphical category
{\Xi^{\times}}
for circuit algebras.

Sections
4.1
and
4.2
provide a review of the category (Feynman) graphs and étale morphisms, first introduced in
[
27
]
, that will underpin the rest of the paper. The interested reader is referred to
[
41
, Sections 3 & 4]
for details and explicit proofs related to the graphical formalism.

Section

4.3
gives a brief description of the operads of wiring diagrams and Brauer diagram category used to describe enriched circuit algebras (see e.g.,
[
3
,
13
,
14
]
and
[
42
]
), and explains their relationship with Feynman graphs. This is purely for context and may be omitted.

4.1.
Graphs and étale morphisms

The following definition is due to Joyal and Kock
[
27
]
(see also
[
30
,
41
,
23
,
24
]
):

Definition 4.1
.

A
(Feynman) graph

\mathcal{G}
is a diagram of finite sets

(4.2)

\mathcal{G}=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 29.38367pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{s}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 98.32634pt\raise 5.15279pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.15279pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern such that
s\colon H\to E
is injective, and
\tau\colon E\to E
is an involution without fixed points.

A
subgraph

\mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{G}
is a triple of subsets
E^{\prime}\subset E,H^{\prime}\subset H,V^{\prime}\subset V
that inherit a graph structure from
\mathcal{G}
.

Elements of
V
are
vertices
of
\mathcal{G}
and elements of
E
are called
edges
of
\mathcal{G}
. The set of
\tau
-orbits in
E
is denoted by
\tilde{E}
.
Elements
h
of the set
H
are
half-edges
of
\mathcal{G}
.

Definition 4.3
.

The set
{E_{\bullet}}\subset E
of
inner edges
of
\mathcal{G}
is the maximal subset of
im(s)\subset E
that is closed under
\tau
. The set of
inner
\tau
-orbits

\tilde{e}\in\widetilde{E}
with
e\in{E_{\bullet}}
is denoted by
\widetilde{{E_{\bullet}}}
.
Elements of the set
E_{0}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}E\setminus im(s)
are
ports
of
\mathcal{G}
.

A
stick component
of a graph
\mathcal{G}
is a pair
\{e,\tau e\}
of edges of
\mathcal{G}
such that
e
and
\tau e
are both ports.

Definition 4.4
.

A graph is
connected
if it cannot be written as a disjoint union of non-empty graphs.
A connected component
\mathcal{H}
of
\mathcal{G}
is a maximal connected subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
.

In particular,
any graph
\mathcal{G}
is the disjoint union of its connected components, and the inclusion
\mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
of a connected component
\mathcal{H}
of
\mathcal{G}
describes a pointwise injective étale morphism.

Example 4.5
.

A subgraph
\mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{G}
of the form
\textstyle{\{e,\tau e\}}
∅
∅
describes a connected component of
\mathcal{G}
if and only if
\{e,\tau e\}
is a stick component of
\mathcal{G}
.

Remark 4.6
.

A graph
\mathcal{G}
can be thought of as a rule for gluing closed intervals along their boundaries and may be realised geometrically as the one-dimensional space
|\mathcal{G}|
, obtained by taking the discrete space
\{*_{v}\}_{v\in V}
, and, for each
e\in E
, a copy
[0,\frac{1}{2}]_{e}
of the interval
[0,\frac{1}{2}]
subject to the identifications
0_{s(h)}\sim*_{t(h)}
for
h\in H
and
(\frac{1}{2})_{e}\sim(\frac{1}{2})_{\tau e}
for all
e\in E
.

Disjoint union commutes with geometric realisation: for all graphs
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
,
|\mathcal{G}\amalg\mathcal{H}|=|\mathcal{G}|\amalg|\mathcal{H}|
. So, a graph
\mathcal{G}
is connected if and only if its realisation
|\mathcal{G}|
is a connected space.

.

(See also

.)

Example 4.8
.

(See also

The
X

{\mathcal{C}_{X}}
associated to a finite set

\textstyle{{\mathcal{C}_{X}}:}
\textstyle{X\amalg X^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\dagger}
\textstyle{X^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
inc
\textstyle{\{*\}.}

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph with vertex and edge sets
V
and
E
respectively. For each vertex
v
, define
\nicefrac{{H}}{{v}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}t^{-1}(v)\subset H
to be the fibre of
t
at
v
, and let
\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}s(\nicefrac{{H}}{{v}})\subset E
.

Definition 4.9
.

Edges in the set
\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}
are said to be
incident on
v
.

The
valency
|v|
of a vertex
v\in V
is given by
|v|\overset{\textup{def}}{=}|\nicefrac{{H}}{{v}}|
and
V_{n}\subset V
is the set of
n
-valent
vertices of
\mathcal{G}
. A vertex
v
is
bivalent
if
|v|=2
and a
bivalent graph
is a graph
\mathcal{G}
with
V=V_{2}
. A vertex
v\in V
with
|v|=0
is called an
isolated vertex
of
\mathcal{G}
.

Bivalent and isolated vertices are particularly important in
Section

6.3
.

Vertex valency induces an
\mathbb{N}
-grading on the edge set
E
of
\mathcal{G}
: For
n\geq 1
, define
H_{n}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}t^{-1}(V_{n})
and
E_{n}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}s(H_{n})
. Then
s(H)=\coprod_{n\geq 1}E_{n}=E\setminus E_{0}
, so

E=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}E_{n}.

Example 4.10
.

The stick graph
(\shortmid)

. So, if

|*|=n
,

, and

Definition 4.11
.

An
étale morphism

f\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
of graphs is a commuting diagram of finite sets

(4.12)

\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{\prime}}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{V^{\prime}}

such that the right-hand square is a pullback. The category of graphs and étale morphisms is denoted by
\mathsf{Gr}
.

It is straightforward to verify that the right hand square in (
4.12
) is a pullback if and only if, for all
v\in V
, the map
\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}\to\nicefrac{{E^{\prime}}}{{f(v)}}
induced by the restriction of the map
V\to V^{\prime}
is bijective. Hence, as the name suggests, étale morphisms describe local isomorphisms.

Example 4.13
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph with edge set
E
. Then
\mathsf{Gr}(\shortmid,\mathcal{G})=\{ch_{e}\}_{e\in E}\cong E
where for each edge
e\in\mathsf{V}
,

1\mapsto e
that

e
.

Pointwise disjoint union defines a symmetric strict monoidal structure on
\mathsf{Gr}
with unit given by the empty graph
\oslash\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\ \hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}\ }}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 34.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ \hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.50002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.50002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 79.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ \hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Example 4.14
.

Let
X
and
Y
be finite sets. The disjoint union
{\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}
of
X
and
Y
-corollas has ports
E_{0}({\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}})=X\amalg Y=E_{0}({\mathcal{C}_{X}})\amalg E_{0}({\mathcal{C}_{Y}})
and no internal edges. The canonical inclusions
\iota_{X}\colon{\mathcal{C}_{X}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}\hookleftarrow{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}\colon\iota_{Y}
are étale.

Example 4.15
.

Up to isomorphism,
(\shortmid)
is the only connected graph with no vertices.
As in
[
41
]
, and following
[
30
]
, a
shrub

\mathcal{S}
is a disjoint union of stick graphs.
Any commuting diagram of the form (
4.12
), where
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{S}
is a shrub, is trivially étale and hence defines a morphism in
\mathsf{Gr}
.

Example 4.16
.

(C.f.
[
42
, Remark 3.12]
.)
Let
\mathsf{\bm{D}}
be the small category
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
. So
\mathsf{Gr}
is a subcategory of
\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{\bm{D}})
.
The endomorphisms
id,\tau\colon(\shortmid)\rightrightarrows(\shortmid)
of
(\shortmid)
in
\mathsf{Gr}
are coequalised in
\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{\bm{D}})
by
*\leftarrow\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}\rightarrow\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
, which is not a graph. So,
id_{(\shortmid)}
and
\tau
do not admit a coequaliser in
\mathsf{Gr}
. This provided a motivating example for
[
41
]
.

The two endomorphisms
id_{\mathcal{W}},\tau_{\mathcal{W}}\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{W}
, viewed as morphisms of presheaves on
\mathsf{\bm{D}}
, have coequaliser
*\leftarrow*\rightarrow*
which is terminal in
\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{\bm{D}})
, and is not a graph. In particular,
\mathsf{Gr}
does not have a terminal object and is therefore not closed under (finite) limits.

As
Example

4.16
shows,
\mathsf{Gr}
does not, in general, admit finite colimits. However, if
\mathcal{G}
is a graph and
e_{1},e_{2}\in E_{0}
are ports of
\mathcal{G}
with
e_{1}\neq\tau e_{2}
, then the colimit
\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e_{2}}
of the parallel morphisms

(4.17)

ch_{e_{1}},ch_{e_{2}}\circ\tau\colon(\shortmid)\rightrightarrows\mathcal{G}

is the graph described by identifying
e_{1}\sim\tau e_{2}
and
e_{2}\sim\tau e_{1}
in the diagram (
4.2
) defining
\mathcal{G}
.

Example 4.18
.

The graph
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}}^{e\ddagger\tau e}
obtained by identifying the ports of the
\mathbf{2}

.

Example 4.19
.

More generally, for
X
a finite set with distinct elements
x

\mathcal{C}_{X}^{x\ddagger y}

\textstyle{(X\setminus\{x,y\})\amalg X^{\dagger}}

\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\{v\},}

where
\tau x^{\dagger}=y^{\dagger}
and
\tau z=z^{\dagger}
for
z\in X\setminus\{x,y\}
.
Graphs of the form
\mathcal{C}_{X}^{x\ddagger y}
encode formal contractions in graphical species.

Since the monoidal structure on
\mathsf{Gr}
is induced by the cocartesian monoidal structure on diagrams of finite sets, colimits in
\mathsf{Gr}
, when they exist, commute with
\amalg
. In particular,
for any graphs
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
and distinct ports

of
\mathcal{G}
,

(4.20)

\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e_{2}}\amalg\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{G}\amalg\mathcal{H})^{e_{1}\ddagger e_{2}}.

Line and wheel graphs.

By
[
41
, Proposition 4.23]
, a connected bivalent graph is isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}^{k}
or
\mathcal{W}^{m}
for some
k,m
.

In general, given ports
e_{1},e_{2}
of a graph
\mathcal{G}
such that
e_{1}\neq\tau e_{2}
, then
\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e_{2}}
is formed by “gluing” the ports
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
(see
Figure

4
). As the following examples shows, the result of forming
\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e_{2}}
is somewhat surprising when
e_{1}=\tau e_{2}
, so
e_{1},e_{2}
are the edges of the same stick component:

Example 4.24
.

Observe that

ch_{1}^{(\shortmid)}\colon(\shortmid)\to(\shortmid)
,
1\mapsto 1
is the identity on
(\shortmid)
and
ch_{2}^{(\shortmid)}\colon(\shortmid)\to(\shortmid)
,
1\mapsto 2
is precisely
\tau
. Hence

ch_{2}^{(\shortmid)}\circ\tau=\tau^{2}=id_{\shortmid},

and therefore

(\shortmid)^{1\ddagger 2}=(\shortmid).

It follows that, if a pair of ports
e_{1},e_{2}=\tau e_{1}\in E_{0}(\mathcal{G})
form a stick component of a graph
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

4.3
), then
\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e_{2}}\cong\mathcal{G}
.

Remark 4.25
.

(See also
[
41
]
, in particular
[
41
, Introduction & Section 6]
.)
It is worth contrasting
Example

4.16
with
Example

4.24
above. Intuitively (graphically), the (formal) coequaliser of
id,\tau\colon(\shortmid)\rightrightarrows(\shortmid)
in
Example

4.16
can be thought of as gluing the two ends of the exceptional edge together to form a nodeless loop, and one may even wish to formally add the diagram
*\leftarrow 0\rightarrow 0
to
\mathsf{Gr}
to encode this operation. However, as
Example

4.24
shows, it is in fact the case that
(\shortmid)^{1\ddagger 2}=(\shortmid)
. (In
Example

6.11
, it will be seen that
(\shortmid)
is also the outcome of “substituting
(\shortmid)
into
\mathcal{W}
”.) Indeed, in
[
23
,
24
]
graphs are defined as in
Definition

4.1
together with additional boundary information and the nodeless loop is described by the same graph as
\shortmid
, but with empty boundary.

This work, like
[
41
]
, takes a different approach, partly informed by the Weber nerve construction. No nodeless loop graph is introduced. Instead contracted units are defined in terms of a map (in the Kleisli category of the monad
\mathbb{D}
,
Section

6
) from the isolated vertex to the exceptional edge
(\shortmid)
. (See also the Introduction,
Section

7.2
and
[
41
, Sections 6 & 7]
.)

Definition 4.26
.

Following
[
23
]
, a graph
embedding
is a morphism
f\in\mathsf{Gr}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
that factors
as

\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e^{\prime}_{1},\dots,e_{k}\dagger e^{\prime}_{k}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}

where the first morphism describes a colimit
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e^{\prime}_{1},\dots,e_{k}\dagger e^{\prime}_{k}}
of pairs of parallel maps

ch_{e_{i}},ch_{e^{\prime}_{i}}\circ\tau\colon(\shortmid)\rightrightarrows\mathcal{G},

(with
e_{1},e^{\prime}_{1},\dots,e_{k},e^{\prime}_{k}
a possibly empty set of distinct ports of
\mathcal{G}
), and the second morphism is a pointwise injection in
\mathsf{Gr}
.

Embeddings form a subcategory of
\mathsf{Gr}
that includes all isomorphisms. They may be thought of as analogous to inclusions of open subsets in topology. The category
\mathsf{Gr}
also admits constructions that play the role of neighbourhoods of points in topology. (See
Remark

4.38
for a description of the Grothendieck topology on
\mathsf{Gr}
. This is discussed in more detail in
[
27
,
41
]
.)

Definition 4.27
.

A
neighbourhood
of a vertex
v\in V
(respectively, an edge
e\in E
) is an embedding
u\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
such that
v
(respectively
e
) is in the image of
u
.

There is a canonical choice of neighbourhood for each vertex
v
and edge
e
of a graph
\mathcal{G}
:

Example 4.28
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph.
For each orbit
\tilde{e}\in\tilde{E}
, the inclusion of the stick graph

{\shortmid_{\tilde{e}}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.06425pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.06425pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\{e,\tau e\}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 41.06425pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.06425pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 76.06425pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 76.06425pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

canonically induces an embedding
\iota_{{\tilde{e}}}\colon({\shortmid_{\tilde{e}}})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
.

Recall that, for each
v\in V
,
\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}s(t^{-1}(v))
is the set of edges incident on
v
. Let
\mathbf{v}=(\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}})^{\dagger}
denote its formal involution.

Then the corolla
{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}
is given by

{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}=\qquad\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.495pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.495pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}{{}{{}{{}{{}{{}{{}}}}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-5.4923pt\raise-4.63547pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 49.22899pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 79.22899pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\nicefrac{{H}}{{v}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.20883pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{s}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.96298pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 105.71645pt\raise 5.15279pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.15279pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 120.5649pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 120.5649pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\{v\}}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

The inclusion
\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}\hookrightarrow E
induces an embedding
\iota_{{v}}^{\mathcal{G}}
or
\iota_{{v}}\colon{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}\to\mathcal{G}
. Observe that, whenever there is an edge
e
such that
e
and
\tau e
are both incident on the same vertex
v
– so
\{e,\tau e\}
forms a
loop
at
v
– then
\iota_{{v}}
is not injective on edges.

If
\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}
is empty – so
{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}
is an isolated vertex – then
{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
is a connected component of
\mathcal{G}
.

For each
v\in V
(respectively,
e\in E
), an embedding
u\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
is a neighbourhood of
v
(respectively
e
) precisely when
\iota_{{v}}\colon{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}\to\mathcal{G}
(respectively
\iota_{{\tilde{e}}}\colon({\shortmid_{\tilde{e}}})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
) factors through
u
.

4.2.
Evaluating graphical species on graphs

The assignments
\lx@sectionsign\mapsto(\shortmid)
,
X\mapsto{\mathcal{C}_{X}}
(for finite sets
X
) define a full inclusion
\Phi\colon{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Gr}
.
Let
\Upsilon\colon\mathsf{Gr}\to\mathsf{GS}
be the induced nerve, given by
\Upsilon\mathcal{G}\colon\mathcal{C}\longmapsto\mathsf{Gr}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{G})
for
\mathcal{C}\in{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
.

Definition 4.29
.

For
\mathcal{G}\in\mathsf{Gr}
, the category
\mathsf{el}{(\Upsilon\mathcal{G})}\cong{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}/\mathcal{G}
is called the
category of elements of
\mathcal{G}
and denoted simply by
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
.

The following lemma is proved in
[
41
, Lemmas 4.16 & 4.24]
and makes precise the intuitive idea that any graph
\mathcal{G}
is obtained by gluing together edges and corollas (the elements of
\mathcal{G}
):

Lemma 4.30
.

For all graphs
\mathcal{G}
,

\mathcal{G}=\mathrm{colim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}}\mathcal{C}
canonically. In other words,
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
is dense in
\mathsf{Gr}
.

If
\mathsf{V}
is a category with all small limits, then the pullback functor
\Phi^{*}\colon\mathsf{psh_{\mathsf{V}}}(\mathsf{Gr})\to\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
along
\Phi\colon{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Gr}
has a right adjoint
\Phi_{*}\colon\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{psh_{\mathsf{V}}}(\mathsf{Gr})
given by

(4.31)

S\longmapsto\left(\mathcal{G}\mapsto\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}}S(\mathcal{C})\right).

Since
\Phi
is fully faithful, so is
\Phi_{*}

[
1
, Exposé ii]
. The same notation will be used for graphical species viewed as (
\mathsf{V}
-valued) presheaves on
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
and for their image as presheaves on
\mathsf{Gr}
.

Example 4.32
.

By (
4.31
),
S(\mathcal{G})\times S(\mathcal{H})=S(\mathcal{G}\amalg\mathcal{H})
for all graphs
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
. In particular,

(4.33)

S_{X}\times S_{Y}=S({\mathcal{C}_{X}})\times S({\mathcal{C}_{Y}})=S({\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}})\text{ for all finite sets }X\text{ and }Y.

So, an external product
\boxtimes
on
S
(
Definition

3.21
) may be defined by a collection of equivariant maps
S({\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}})\to S_{X\amalg Y}
.

Example 4.34
.

Let
X
be a finite set with distinct elements
x
and
y
.
By (
3.16
),
S_{X}^{x\ddagger y}\in\mathsf{V}
is the equaliser of the morphisms
S(ch_{x}),S(ch_{y}\circ\tau)\colon S_{X}\rightrightarrows S_{\lx@sectionsign}
. Since the graph
\mathcal{C}_{X}^{x\ddagger y}
is defined as the coequaliser of the morphisms
ch_{x},ch_{y}\circ\tau\colon(\shortmid)\to{\mathcal{C}_{X}}
(see
Example

4.19
), it follows from (
4.31
) that

(4.35)

S^{x\ddagger y}_{X}=S(\mathcal{C}_{X}^{x\ddagger y}).

Hence, a contraction
\zeta
on a graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{V}
(
Definition

3.17
) is defined by a collection of maps
S(\mathcal{C}^{x\ddagger y}_{X})\to S_{X\setminus\{x,y\}}
where
X
is a finite set with distinct elements
x,y\in X
.

Likewise, if
X,Y
are finite sets with elements
x\in X
,
y\in Y
, then by (
3.12
,

(4.36)

(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x\ddagger y}=(S({\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}))^{x\ddagger y}=S(({\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}})^{x\ddagger y}),

since
({\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}})^{x\ddagger y}
is the coequaliser of the morphisms
ch_{x},ch_{y}\circ\tau\colon(\shortmid)\to{\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}
.

So, a multiplication
\diamond
on
S
(
Definition

3.14
) is defined by a collection of maps
S(({\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}})^{x\ddagger y})\to S_{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x,y\}}
where
X,Y
are finite sets and
x\in X,y\in Y
.

Definition 4.37
.

When
\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{Set}
and
S\in\mathsf{GS}
, an element
\alpha\in S(\mathcal{G})
is called an
S
-structure on
\mathcal{G}
. Objects of the category
\mathsf{Gr}(S)
, of
S
-structured graphs
, are pairs
(\mathcal{G},\alpha)
with
\alpha\in S(\mathcal{G})
, and morphisms
(\mathcal{G},\alpha)\to(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{Gr}(S)
are given by morphisms
f\in\mathsf{Gr}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
such that
S(f)(\alpha^{\prime})=\alpha
.

By
Lemma

4.30
,
\mathsf{Gr}
may be viewed as a full subcategory of
\mathsf{GS}
and for graphs
\mathcal{G}\in\mathsf{Gr}
, I will write
\mathcal{G}\in\mathsf{GS}
, rather than
\Upsilon\mathcal{G}\in\mathsf{GS}
where there is no risk of confusion.

Remark 4.38
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph. Families of jointly surjective morphisms define the covers at
\mathcal{G}
for a canonical
étale topology

J
on
\mathsf{Gr}
. Since
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
refines every cover at
\mathcal{G}
, the category
\mathsf{GS}
of graphical species in
\mathsf{Set}
is equivalent to the category
\mathsf{sh}(\mathsf{Gr},J)
of étale sheaves on
\mathsf{Gr}
. It follows, in particular, that
there is a canonical isomorphism

S(\mathcal{G})\cong\mathsf{GS}(\Upsilon\mathcal{G},S)
. Hence,
\mathsf{Gr}(S)
is canonically isomorphic to the slice category
\mathsf{Gr}/S
.

Example 4.39
.

The terminal oriented graphical species
Di\in\mathsf{GS}
was defined in
Example

3.7
.
By
Remark

4.38
, a
Di
-structure
\phi\in\mathsf{GS}(\mathcal{G},Di)
on a graph
\mathcal{G}
is given by a bijection
\{e,\tau e\}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathfrak{Di}
for each edge orbit
\{e,\tau e\}
of
\mathcal{G}
. In other words,
\phi
defines an orientation on
\mathcal{G}
. Hence, the
category
\mathsf{OGr}\cong\mathsf{Gr}(Di)
of directed graphs
is defined as the slice category
\mathsf{Gr}/Di
. This is a full subcategory of
\mathsf{OGS}=\mathsf{GS}/Di
by
Lemma

4.30
and each directed graph
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}=(\mathcal{G},\phi)\in\mathsf{OGr}
is canonically the colimit of its induced oriented element category
\mathsf{el}_{Di}{(\tilde{\mathcal{G}})}
obtained by pulling back the orientation
\phi
on
\mathcal{G}
along each element
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
. (See also
[
41
, Section 4.5]
.)

In particular, if
\tilde{S}\colon{\mathsf{el}{(Di)}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{V}
is an oriented graphical species in
\mathsf{V}
corresponding to a pair
(S,\gamma)\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}/Di^{\mathsf{V}}
, then
\tilde{S}
extends to a presheaf on
\mathsf{OGr}
such that, for all graphs
\mathcal{G}\in\mathsf{Gr}
,

S(\mathcal{G})\cong\coprod_{\phi\in\mathfrak{Di}(\mathcal{G})}\tilde{S}(\mathcal{G},\phi).

4.3.
Relationship between graphs and Brauer diagrams and wiring diagrams

In
Theorem

7.9
, circuit algebras internal to a category
\mathsf{V}
will be constructed as algebras for a composite monad
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
on
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
that is described in terms of graphs in
\mathsf{Gr}
. By contrast, enriched circuit algebras are usually defined as algebras over operads of wiring diagrams (see e.g.,
[
3
,
12
,
25
,
50
,
14
]
and
[
42
]
).

In
[
42
, Section 4]
, the operad
\mathrm{WD}
of (monochrome unoriented) wiring diagrams is defined using the symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{BD}
of Brauer diagrams, used in the representation theory of orthogonal and symplectic groups
[
34
,
44
,
42
]
. In fact, (enriched) circuit algebras may equivalently be described by symmetric lax monoidal functors from categories of (coloured) Brauer diagrams.

A partial overview of how different variations of circuit algebras are associated, via Schur-Weyl duality, to group representations, and how they relate to the composite circuit algebra monad
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
is provided in
[
42
, Introduction]
. This short section reviews the definitions of the category
\mathsf{BD}
and operad
\mathrm{WD}
, describes how wiring diagrams are related to graphs as in
Definition

4.1
, and indicates how composition of wiring diagrams is encoded by the monads
\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{T}
introduced in the following Sections
5
-
7.2
.

Objects of
\mathsf{BD}
are natural numbers. Morphisms are
(monochrome, unoriented) Brauer diagrams
, where a Brauer diagram in
f\colon m\to n
is a pair
f=(\tau,\mathfrak{k})
such that

\mathfrak{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
is a natural number and
\tau
is a fixed-point free involution (or
matching
) on
\mathbf{m}\amalg\mathbf{n}
(as usual,
\mathbf{n}=\{1,\dots,n\}
for all
n\geq 1
and
\mathbf{0}=\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
).

A Brauer diagram
(\tau,\mathfrak{k})\colon m\to n
may be visualised, as in
Figure

6
(b) by
\mathfrak{k}
closed loops next to a univalent graph that comprises of
a row of
n
vertices bijectively labelled by
\mathbf{n}
above a row of
m
vertices labelled by
\mathbf{m}
, such that there is an edge connecting vertices
x
and
y
in
\mathbf{m}\amalg\mathbf{n}
if and only if
x=\tau y
.
The monoidal product
\oplus
on
\mathsf{BD}
is given by
f_{1}\oplus f_{2}=(\tau_{1}\amalg\tau_{2},\mathfrak{k}_{1}+\mathfrak{k}_{2})\in\mathsf{BD}(m_{1}+m_{2},n_{1}+n_{2})
for

f_{1}=(\tau_{1},\mathfrak{k}_{1})\in\mathsf{BD}(m_{1},n_{1})
and
f_{2}=(\tau_{2},\mathfrak{k}_{2})\in\mathsf{BD}(m_{2},n_{2})
. It is represented graphically by juxtaposition.
Categorical composition is described by “diagram stacking” as in
Figure

6
(see also
[
42
, Section 3]
).

(a)
(b)

Figure 6.
(a) Composition of Brauer diagrams
f=(\tau_{f},0)\in\mathsf{BD}(5,7)
and
g=(\tau_{g},0)\in\mathsf{BD}(7,3)
(b) the resulting Brauer diagram
gf=(\tau_{g}\tau_{f},1)\in\mathsf{BD}(5,3)
with a single loop formed in the composition.

The operad
\mathrm{WD}
is the
\mathbb{N}
-coloured
operad underlying
\mathsf{BD}
. This means that, for each tuple of natural numbers
(m_{1},\dots,m_{k};n)
, the set of
(monochrome) wiring diagrams of type
(m_{1},\dots,m_{k};n)
is given by
\mathrm{WD}(m_{1},\dots,m_{k};n)\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{BD}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i};n)
. For
f\in\mathsf{BD}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i};n)
, the corresponding wiring diagram is denoted by
\overline{f}\in\mathrm{WD}(m_{1},\dots,m_{k};n)
. The (strict) symmetric monoidal category structure on
\mathsf{BD}
makes
\mathrm{WD}
into an
\mathbb{N}
-coloured operad (see
[
42
, Section 4]
) with composition

\gamma\colon\mathrm{WD}(m_{1},\dots,m_{k};n)\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{WD}(l_{i,1},\dots,l_{i,k_{i}};m_{i})\to\mathrm{WD}(l_{1,1},\dots,l_{k,k_{i}};n)

given by

\gamma\left(\overline{g},(\overline{f}_{i})_{i}\right)\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\overline{\left(g\circ(f_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus f_{n})\right)}.

It follows, in particular, that an algebra over
\mathrm{WD}
(a monochrome circuit algebra) is, equivalently, a lax monoidal functor from
\mathsf{BD}
(see
[
42
, Sections 3 & 4]
).

Remark 4.40
.

Extending the Brauer’s 1937 work
[
8
]
, categories of (oriented) Brauer diagrams have been used to study the representation theory of the (general linear) orthogonal and symplectic groups (see, e.g.,
[
34
,
43
,
44
]
).

Building on the wheeled prop characterisation of algebras for the general linear groups in
[
15
]
,
[
42
, Theorem 6.3]
characterises algebras over the finite dimensional orthogonal and symplectic groups as (enriched) circuit algebras satisfying two simple relations.

The relationship between (monochrome) wiring diagrams and graphs is straightforward: If a monochrome wiring diagram
\overline{f}\in\mathrm{WD}(m_{1},\dots,m_{k};n)
is induced by a Brauer diagram
f\in\mathsf{BD}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i},n)
of the form
f=(\tau_{f},0)
(so
f
has no closed loops), then
f
describes a graph:

(4.41)

\textstyle{\left(\mathbf{n}\amalg(\coprod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{m}_{i})\right)}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}}
\textstyle{{\coprod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{m}_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
inc
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\mathbf{k}}

where
p\colon\coprod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{m}_{i}\to\mathbf{k}
is the canonical projection.

Example 4.42
.

When
X=\mathbf{n}
, the corolla
{\mathcal{C}_{X}}
defined in
Example

4.8
corresponds by (
4.41
) to the identity wiring diagram
\overline{id_{n}}\in\mathrm{WD}(\mathbf{n};\mathbf{n})
.

By
[
34
, Theorem 2.6]
or
[
2
, Proposition 2.15]
, the category
\mathsf{BD}
is generated, under composition and monoidal product, by the Brauer diagrams
\sigma_{\mathbf{2}}\in\mathsf{BD}(2,2)
(the non-identity bijection between source and target sets that generates the symmetry in
\mathsf{BD}
) and
id_{1}\in\mathsf{BD}(1,1)
,
\cap\in\mathsf{BD}(2,0)
and
\cup\in\mathsf{BD}(0,2)
.

Example 4.43
.

With the exception of
\sigma_{\mathbf{2}}
, each of the generating morphisms of
\mathsf{BD}
has the form
(\tau_{\mathbf{2}},0)
, where
\tau_{\mathbf{2}}
is the unique matching on the two-element set. Hence
\tau_{\mathbf{2}}
induces distinct wiring diagrams

\overline{id_{1}}\in\mathrm{WD}(1;1)
,
\overline{\cup}\in\mathrm{WD}(-;2)
and
\overline{\cap}_{(\mathbf{2};\mathbf{0})}\in\mathrm{WD}(2;0)
and
\overline{\cap}_{(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1};\mathbf{0})}\in WD(1,1;0)
. By (
4.41
), these describe the four graphs with a single edge orbit from
Example

4.7
:

(a)

for
\overline{id_{1}}\in\mathrm{WD}(1;1)
, the corresponding graph is the
\mathbf{1}
-corolla

\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{1}}
(
Figure

3
(a));

(b)

for
\overline{\cup}\in\mathrm{WD}(-;2)
, the corresponding graph is the
stick graph

(\shortmid)
(
Figure

3
(b));

(c)

for
\overline{\cap}_{(\mathbf{2};\mathbf{0})}\in\mathrm{WD}(2;0)
, the corresponding graph is the
wheel graph

\mathcal{W}

(
Figure

3
(c));

(d)

for
\overline{\cap}_{(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1};\mathbf{0})}\in WD(1,1;0)
, the corresponding graph is given by
Example

4.7
(d) (
Figure

3
(d)).

Remark 4.44
.

Wiring diagrams are, essentially, symmetric versions of Jones’s
planar diagrams

[
26
]
. As such, they are commonly represented, not by Brauer diagrams, but in terms of immersions of compact 1-manifolds in punctured discs as in
Figure

7
(see e.g.,
[
13
]
and
[
42
, Section 4.2]
for details).

Consider the punctured disc representation of the wiring diagram
\overline{g}
in
Figure

7
. It is clear that, by shrinking the punctures to points, and deleting the outer disc, we obtain a representation of a graph with
3
vertices of degree
2,4
and
3
respectively, and
3
ports. This corresponds, via (
4.41
), to the geometric realisation (
Remark

4.6
) of a Feynman graph. This also holds for each of the wiring diagrams
\overline{f_{i}}
. Note, however, that a loop is formed in the composition
\overline{\left(g\circ(f_{1}\oplus f_{2}\oplus f_{3})\right)}
, so shrinking the punctures to points in this diagram is not sufficient to obtain a Feynman graph.

\gamma

Figure 7.
Brauer diagram and punctured disc representations of the same composition of wiring diagrams.

Inclusions of connected components in
\mathsf{Gr}
describe factors of monoidal products in
\mathsf{BD}
. But, in general, morphisms in
\mathsf{Gr}
do not admit a nice description in terms of
\mathsf{BD}
(or
\mathrm{WD}
). Nonetheless, since the category
{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}
of circuit algebras in
\mathsf{Set}
is equivalent to the category
\bm{\mathsf{CA}}
described in
[
42
]
, and objects of
\bm{\mathsf{CA}}
are algebras over operads of wiring diagrams
[
42
, Section 4]
, there must be constructions on graphs that play the role of operadic composition, and are able to encode the formation of closed loops, in
\mathrm{WD}
.

Remark 4.45
.

(See also Remarks
4.46
and
5.3
)
The subcategory
\mathsf{dBD}\subset\mathsf{BD}
of
downward Brauer diagrams
is generated by
\sigma_{\mathbf{2}}=(\{s_{1}\mapsto t_{2},s_{2}\mapsto t_{1}\},0)\in\mathsf{BD}(2,2)
,
id_{1}\in\mathsf{BD}(1,1)
and
\cap\in\mathsf{BD}(2,0)
(but not
\cup\in\mathsf{BD}(0,2)
). Morphisms in
\mathsf{dBD}(m,n)
have the form
f=(\tau,0)
where, for all
t_{i}\in\mathfrak{T}(f)
,
\tau(t_{i})\in Sf
. In particular, Brauer diagrams in
\mathsf{dBD}
are “loop-free".

By
[
44
]
finite-length (vector-space valued) functors from
\mathsf{dBD}
are equivalent to representations of the infinite dimensional orthogonal (or symplectic) group, and representations of the infinite dimensional general linear group are described by functors from the category of downward
oriented
Brauer diagrams.

Punctured disc style representations of downward wiring diagrams are precisely those that do not include loops or wires that meet the outer disc at both ends.

In
[
42
, Section 4]
, nonunital circuit algebras are defined as lax symmetric monoidal functors from (coloured versions of)
\mathsf{dBD}
. In the category
\mathsf{Set}
, these are equivalent to algebras for the monad
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}=\mathbb{L}\mathbb{T}
described in
Section

5
, and composition of downward wiring diagrams is given by taking colimits of nondegenerate graphs of graphs as described in
Section

5.1
(see also
Remark

5.3
).

In particular, the categories of algebras for the infinite orthogonal, symplectic and general linear groups admit descriptions in terms of nonunital circuit algebras. (See the discussions in
[
42
, Introduction & Section 6.2]
, and
[
42
, Theorem 6.13]
in particular.)

Remark 4.46
.

(See also Remarks
4.45
and
5.3
)
To describe units for circuit algebras, the morphism
\cup\in\mathsf{BD}(0,2)
is also required. This generates punctured disc representations that may include loops and wires that meet the outer disc at both ends. Composition of general wiring diagrams – that describes operations of unital circuit algebras
[
3
,
13
,
41
]
– is related to taking colimits of (possibly degenerate) graphs of graphs (
Section

5.1
). The graphical combinatorics of this are encoded by the unit monad
\mathbb{D}
that will be described in
Section

6
and the iterated distributive laws that will be described in
Section

7.2
.

Remark 4.47
.

The (internal) construction of circuit algebras discussed in this work characterises
all
circuit algebras as algebras for a single monad on
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
, with special cases, such as wheeled props (oriented circuit algebras) obtained as subcategories or via slice constructions. By contrast, different versions of (enriched) circuit algebras – such as oriented or nonoriented – are algebras over distinct (different coloured) operads of wiring diagrams.

New categories of Brauer diagrams – and hence new operads of wiring diagrams – may be obtained by adding colours to the connected components of the underlying graphs as in
[
42
, Section 3.2]
. In particular, the category
\mathsf{OBD}
of
oriented monochrome diagrams
, whose objects are elements of the monoid
\small{\mathsf{list}}\{{\mathrm{in}},{\mathrm{out}}\}
, may be defined similarly to
\mathsf{BD}
by orienting the edges of the Brauer diagrams. (See
[
42
, Section 3.2]
for a precise definition in terms of the palette
(\mathfrak{Di},\sigma_{\mathfrak{Di}})
defined in
Example

3.7
.) This category is important in the representation theory of general linear groups
[
34
,
44
,
42
]
.

5.
A monad for nonunital circuit algebras

This section gives a short outline, closely related to
[
27
]
and
[
41
, Section 5]
, of the construction of the nonunital circuit algebra monad
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
on
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
where, as usual, the category
\mathsf{V}
is assumed to have enough limits and colimits for all the given constructions.

5.1.
Gluing constructions and labelled graphs

As shown in
Example

4.16
, the graph category
\mathsf{Gr}
is not closed under finite colimits. However, there is a class of diagrams in
\mathsf{Gr}
that always admit colimits, and are central to the definition of the nonunital circuit algebra monad
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
in
Section

5.2
.

The following terminology is based on
[
30
]
:

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph. A
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
is a functor
\mathbf{\Gamma}\colon\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr}
(or
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{G}}
) such that

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{\Gamma}(a)=(\shortmid)&\text{ for all }(\shortmid,a)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})},\\
E_{0}(\mathbf{\Gamma}(b))=X&\text{ for all }({\mathcal{C}_{X}},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})},\end{array}

and, for all
(\mathcal{C}_{X_{b}},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
and all
x\in X_{b}
,

\mathbf{\Gamma}(ch_{x})=ch^{\mathbf{\Gamma}(b)}_{x}\in\mathsf{Gr}(\shortmid,\mathbf{\Gamma}(b)).

A
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}\colon\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr}
is
nondegenerate
if, for all
v\in V
,
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\iota_{{v}})
has no stick components. Otherwise,
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is called
degenerate
.

Figure 8.
A
\mathcal{G}
shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}
describes
graph substitution
in which each vertex
v
of
\mathcal{G}
is replaced by a graph
\mathcal{G}_{v}
according to a bijection
E_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{v})\xrightarrow{\cong}(\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}})^{\dagger}
. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is nondegenerate, then taking its colimit corresponds to erasing the inner (blue) nesting.

A nondegenerate
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs may be viewed informally as a rule for substituting graphs into vertices of
\mathcal{G}
as in
Figure

8
.

Proposition 5.2
.

A
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}
admits a colimit
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})
in
\mathsf{Gr}
, and, for each
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
, the universal map
b_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\colon\mathbf{\Gamma}(b)\to{\mathbf{\Gamma}}({\mathcal{G}})
is an embedding in
\mathsf{Gr}
.

If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is nondegenerate and
\mathbf{\Gamma}(b)\neq\oslash
for all
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
, then

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
describes a bijection
E({\mathbf{\Gamma}}({\mathcal{G}}))\cong E(\mathcal{G})\amalg\coprod_{v\in V}{E_{\bullet}}(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\iota_{{v}}))
of sets;

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
induces an identity
E_{0}(\mathcal{G})\xrightarrow{=}E_{0}({\mathbf{\Gamma}}({\mathcal{G}}))
of ports;

•

there is a canonical surjective map
V(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G}))\twoheadrightarrow V(\mathcal{G})
induced by the embeddings
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\iota_{{v}})\colon\mathbf{\Gamma}(\iota_{{v}})\to\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})
for each vertex
v
of
\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is nondegenerate, then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
admits a colimit
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})
in
\mathsf{Gr}
by
[
41
, Proposition 5.16]
. In case
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is degenerate, the existence of a colimit in
\mathsf{Gr}
follows from
[
41
, Proposition 7.15]
.

The other statements follow directly from
[
41
, Corollary 5.19]
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

(See also Remarks
4.45
and
4.46
.) By
Proposition

3.27
, there is an equivalence of categories between (internal) circuit algebras in
\mathsf{Set}
and
algebras for operads of wiring diagrams. Under this equivalence, graphs of graphs play the role of operadic composition of wiring diagrams.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph with
n
ports,
k
vertices, and such that each vertex
v_{i}\in V
has
m_{i}=|\nicefrac{{E}}{{v_{i}}}|
adjacent edges. Up to orderings,
\mathcal{G}
describes a wiring diagram
\overline{g}\in\mathrm{WD}(\mathbf{m}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{m}_{k};\mathbf{n})
with no closed components. A
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}
such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(b)\neq\oslash
for all
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
describes a choice of
k
wiring diagrams
\overline{f}^{i}\in\mathrm{WD}(\bm{l}_{1},\dots,\bm{l}_{k_{i}};\mathbf{m}_{i})
for
(l_{1},\dots,l_{k_{i}})\in\small{\mathsf{list}}(\mathbb{N})
and
1\leq i\leq k
.

Non-degeneracy of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
corresponds to the condition, discussed in
[
42
, Remark 3.12]
, that, for each
\overline{f}^{i}
in the composition, the underlying morphism
f^{i}
of Brauer diagrams is a morphism of the downward Brauer category
f^{i}\in\mathsf{dBD}(l_{1}+\dots+l_{k_{i}},m_{i})
.
If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is nondegenerate, then its colimit
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})
is described by the composition
\gamma\left(\overline{g},(\overline{f}_{i})_{i}\right)
in
\mathrm{WD}
. (Colimits of degenerate graphs of graphs are discussed in
Section

6.3
.)

Example 5.4
.

By
Lemma

4.30
, every graph
\mathcal{G}
is the colimit of the (nondegenerate)
identity
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs

\mathbf{I}^{\mathcal{G}}
given by the forgetful functor
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr}
,
(\mathcal{C},b)\mapsto\mathcal{C}
.

The identity
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs corresponds, as in
Remark

5.3
to a composition of wiring diagrams of the form
\gamma\left(\overline{g},(\overline{id}_{m_{i}})_{i=1}^{k}\right)
.

Definition 5.5
.

The category
\mathsf{Gr}^{(\mathcal{G})}
of
nondegenerate
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graphs of graphs
is the full subcategory of nondegenerate
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graphs of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}
in the category of functors
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr}
.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
X
be a finite set. A generalised
X
-graph
(henceforth
X
-graph) is a
{\mathcal{C}_{X}}
-shaped graph of graphs. An
X
-graph is
admissible
if it is nondegenerate.

The maximal subgroupoid
\mathrm{Core}(\mathsf{Gr}^{({\mathcal{C}_{X}})})\subset\mathsf{Gr}^{({\mathcal{C}_{X}})}
is called the
groupoid of (admissible)
X
-graphs
and
X
-isomorphisms
and is denoted by
X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}
.

More generally, for any graph
\mathcal{G}
and any
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
,
[b]{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}
denotes the groupoid of nondegenerate
\mathcal{C}
-shaped graphs of graphs and isomorphisms.

Hence, a generalised
X
-graph is given by a pair
\mathcal{X}=(\mathcal{G},\rho)
, where
\mathcal{G}
is a graph and
\rho\colon E_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}X
. An
X
-graph is
admissible
if it has no stick components, and an
X
-isomorphism in
X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}^{\prime})
is an isomorphism
g\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{Gr}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
that preserves the
X
-labelling:
\rho^{\prime}\circ g_{E_{0}}=\rho\colon E_{0}\to X.

Example 5.7
.

For any graph
\mathcal{G}
, a (nondegenerate)
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}\colon\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr}
is precisely a choice of (admissible)
X_{b}
-graph
\mathcal{X}_{b}
for each
({\mathcal{C}_{X_{b}}},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
.

Let
S
be a graphical species in
\mathsf{V}
, and
\mathcal{G}
a graph. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}\colon\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr}
is a nondegenerate
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs with colimit
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})
in
\mathsf{Gr}
, then the composition

\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathsf{Gr}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{S}\mathsf{V}

defines a functor
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{V}
with limit
S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G}))
.

Example 5.8
.

Recall
Definition

4.37
. If
S
is a graphical species in
\mathsf{Set}
and
\mathcal{G}
is any graph, then a nondegenerate
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and choice of
S
-structure
\alpha\in S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G}))
determines a functor
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr}(S)
– called a
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of
S
-structured graphs
– by
b\mapsto S(b_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})(\alpha)
(where, for all
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
,
b_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is the universal map described in
Proposition

5.2
).

5.2.
Nonunital circuit algebras

It is now straightforward to construct a monad
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}=({T^{\times}},{\mu^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}}},{\eta^{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}})
on
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
, whose algebras are nonunital circuit algebras in
\mathsf{V}
.

The underlying endofunctor
{T^{\times}}={T^{\times}}_{\mathsf{V}}\colon\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}\to\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
is defined, for all
S\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
, by

(5.9)

\begin{array}[]{llll}{T^{\times}}S_{\lx@sectionsign}&=&S_{\lx@sectionsign},&\\
{T^{\times}}S_{X}&=&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}}S(\mathcal{X})&\text{ for all finite sets }X.\end{array}

This acts on isomorphisms in
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
,
{T^{\times}}S
acts by relabelling graph ports. Let
g\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
be a port-preserving isomorphism in
X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}
, and for each
x\in X
, let
ch_{x}^{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{Gr}(\shortmid,\mathcal{G})
be the map
ch_{\rho^{-1}(x)}
defined by
1\mapsto\rho^{-1}(x)\in E_{0}(\mathcal{G})
.
Then,

S(g\circ ch^{\mathcal{X}}_{x})=S(ch^{\mathcal{X}}_{x})S(g)=S(ch^{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}_{x}).

Hence, the projections
{T^{\times}}S(ch_{x})
induced by

S({ch^{\mathcal{X}}_{x}})\colon S(\mathcal{X})\to S(\shortmid)=S_{\lx@sectionsign}
,
are well-defined. So
{T^{\times}}S
extends to all morphisms in
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
and therefore defines a graphical species in
\mathsf{V}
. The assignment
S\mapsto{T^{\times}}S
is clearly natural on
S\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
, and so
{T^{\times}}
defines an endofunctor on
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

As in
Proposition

5.2
, for an
X
-graph
\mathcal{X}
and nondegenerate
\mathcal{X}
-shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}
in
\mathsf{Gr}^{(\mathcal{X})}
, let
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X})
denote the colimit of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
in
\mathsf{Gr}^{(\mathcal{X})}
.

Lemma 5.10
.

For any graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{V}
and any finite set
X
, there is a canonical isomorphism

({{T^{\times}}})^{2}S_{X}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}\left(\mathrm{lim}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{Gr}^{(\mathcal{X})}}S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X}))\right).

Proof.

By definition, for all finite sets
X
,

\begin{array}[]{lll}({{T^{\times}}})^{2}S_{X}&=&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}{T^{\times}}S(\mathcal{X})\\
&=&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{X})}}{T^{\times}}S(\mathcal{C})\\
&=&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{X})}}\mathrm{colim}_{Y\in[b]{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}S(\mathcal{Y}).\end{array}

Since
[b]{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}
is a groupoid by definition,
\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{Y}\in[b]{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}S(\mathcal{Y})=\mathrm{lim}_{\mathcal{Y}\in[b]{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}S(\mathcal{Y})
for all
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{X})}
.
By
Definition

5.5
,

\mathrm{lim}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{Gr}^{(\mathcal{X})}}S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X}))\cong\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{X})}}\left(\mathrm{lim}_{\mathcal{Y}\in[b]{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}S(\mathcal{Y})\right)

canonically, and therefore, by the universal property of (co)limits, there is a unique isomorphism

(5.11)

\begin{array}[]{lll}({T^{\times}})^{2}S_{X}&\cong&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}\left(\mathrm{lim}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{Gr}^{(\mathcal{X})}}S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X}))\right).\end{array}

∎

By
Proposition

5.2
, each
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X})
inherits the structure of an
X
-graph from
\mathcal{X}
. Hence, by an application of
Lemma

5.10
, the assignment
(\mathcal{X},\mathbf{\Gamma})\to\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X})
describes a
\mathsf{V}
-morphism
\mu^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}}S_{X}\in E\left(({{T^{\times}}})^{2}S_{X},{T^{\times}}S_{X}\right)
in
\mathsf{V}
given by

(5.12)

\mu^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}}S_{X}\colon({T^{\times}})^{2}S_{X}=\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}\left(\mathrm{lim}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{Gr}^{(\mathcal{X})}}S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X}))\right)\to\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}S(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})={T^{\times}}S_{X}.

This is natural in
X
and in
S
and hence defines a natural transformation
\mu^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}}\colon({T^{\times}})^{2}\Rightarrow{T^{\times}}
.

The inclusion
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Gr}
induces a natural transformation
{\eta^{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}}\colon id_{\mathsf{GS}}\Rightarrow{T^{\times}}
,
S_{X}\xrightarrow{=}S({\mathcal{C}_{X}})\to{T^{\times}}S_{X}
that provides a unit for the endofunctor
{T^{\times}}
on
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
. It is straightforward to verify that the triple
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}=({T^{\times}},\mu^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}},\eta^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}})
defines a monad.

Example 5.13
.

For
S\in\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}
,
{T^{\times}}S
admits a canonical nonunital circuit algebra structure:

Disjoint union of graphs induces an external product
\boxtimes
on
{T^{\times}}S
. For, let
X,Y
be finite sets and let
\mathcal{X}
be an
X
-graph and
\mathcal{Y}
a
Y
-graph.
Then, the following diagram – in which the unlabelled maps are the defining morphisms for
{T^{\times}}
– commutes in
\mathsf{V}
and describes an external product
\boxtimes
on
{T^{\times}}S
:

\textstyle{S(\mathcal{X}\amalg\mathcal{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S(\mathcal{X})\times S(\mathcal{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{T^{\times}}S_{X}\times{T^{\times}}S_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxtimes}
\textstyle{{T^{\times}}S({\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg\mathcal{C}_{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({T^{\times}})^{2}S_{X\amalg Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}}}
\textstyle{{T^{\times}}S_{X\amalg Y}.}

Since
\oslash\in\mathbf{0}{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}
, there is a morphism
*=S(\oslash)\to{T^{\times}}S_{\mathbf{0}}
from the terminal object
*
in
\mathsf{V}
. This provides an external unit for
\boxtimes
.

Similarly, the contraction
\zeta
on
{T^{\times}}S
is induced by commuting diagrams of the form

\textstyle{S(\mathcal{X}^{x\ddagger y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{T^{\times}}S(\mathcal{C}^{x\ddagger y}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{X}^{x\ddagger y}}
\textstyle{({T^{\times}})^{2}S_{X\setminus\{x,y\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}}}
\textstyle{{T^{\times}}S_{X\setminus\{x,y\}}}

where
X
is a finite set with distinct elements
x,y\in X
, and
\mathcal{X}
an
X
-graph.

It follows immediately from the definitions (and the diagrams in the proof of
Proposition

5.14
below) that
\boxtimes
and
\zeta
satisfy (C1)-(C3) of
Definition

3.25
.

Proposition 5.14
.

There is a canonical isomorphism of categories
\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}\cong{\bm{\mathfrak{CA}}}^{-}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

Proof.

Let
(A,h)
be an algebra for
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
.

For finite sets
X
and
Y
, the morphism
\boxtimes_{X,Y}\colon A_{X}\times A_{Y}\to A_{X\amalg Y}
in
\mathsf{V}
is obtained by composing the structure morphism
A_{X}\times A_{Y}=A(\mathcal{C}_{X}\amalg\mathcal{C}_{Y})\to{{T^{\times}}}A_{X\amalg Y}
with
{h}\colon{{T^{\times}}}A_{X\amalg Y}\to A_{X\amalg Y}
. Moreover, the composition

*=A(\oslash)\to{T^{\times}}A_{\mathbf{0}}\xrightarrow{h}A_{\mathbf{0}}
provides a unit for the external product
\boxtimes
so defined.

If a finite set
X
has distinct elements
x
and
y
, then we may define a morphism
\zeta_{X}^{x\ddagger y}\colon A_{X}\to A_{X\setminus\{x,y\}}
in
\mathsf{V}
by composing

A_{X}^{x\ddagger y}=A(\mathcal{C}_{X}^{x\ddagger y})\to{{T^{\times}}}A_{X\setminus\{x,y\}}
with
h
.

The proof that
(A,\boxtimes,\zeta)
satisfies the axioms for a circuit algebra proceeds by representing each side of the equations in (C1)-(C3) of
Definition

3.25
in terms of graph of graphs, and then showing that these have the same colimit in
\mathsf{Gr}
.

For (C1), the external product
\boxtimes_{X,Y}
is represented by the graph
\mathcal{C}_{X}\amalg\mathcal{C}_{Y}
, and so
\boxtimes_{X\amalg Y,Z}\circ(\boxtimes_{X,Y}\ \times\ id_{A_{Z}})
is represented by the
\mathcal{C}_{X\amalg Y}\amalg\mathcal{C}_{Z}
-shaped graphs of graphs
\left(\iota_{X\amalg Y}\mapsto{\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}},\ \iota_{Z}\mapsto{\mathcal{C}_{Z}}\right)
. This has
colimit
{\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Z}}
which is also the colimit of the
{\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg\mathcal{C}_{Y\amalg Z}
-shaped graph of graphs

Conversely, let
(S,\boxtimes,\zeta)
be a nonunital circuit algebra. For finite sets
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
, let
\boxtimes_{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}}:\prod_{i=1}^{n}S_{X_{i}}\to S_{\coprod_{i}X_{i}}
be the obvious morphism induced by
\boxtimes
. This is well defined since
(S,\boxtimes,\zeta)
satisfies (C1)-(C3). Recall from
Section

3.4
that, if
x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{k},y_{k}
are mutually distinct elements of
\coprod_{i}X_{i}
, then
(\prod_{i}S_{X_{i}})^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}
is the limit of the diagram of parallel morphisms

\boxtimes_{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}}\circ S(ch_{x_{i}}),\boxtimes_{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}}\circ S(ch_{y_{i}}\circ\tau)\colon\prod_{i=1}^{n}S_{X_{i}}\to S_{\coprod_{i}X_{i}}\to S_{\lx@sectionsign},

and that this is independent of the ordering of the pairs
(x_{i},y_{i})
.

Let
X
be a finite set and let
\mathcal{X}
be an admissible

X
-graph. For each

\coprod_{v}Z_{v}=X\amalg\{y_{1},z_{1},\dots,y_{k},z_{k}\}
.

Since
\mathcal{X}

has no stick components,

(5.15)

S(\mathcal{X})=\left(\prod_{v\in V(\mathcal{X})}S_{Z_{v}}\right)^{y_{1}\ddagger z_{1},\dots,y_{k}\ddagger z_{k}}.

In particular, the morphism
\prod_{v}S_{Z_{v}}\to S_{(\coprod_{v}Z_{v})}
induces a morphism

\boxtimes^{V}\colon S(\mathcal{X})\to S_{\coprod_{v\in V}Z_{v}}^{y_{1}\ddagger z_{1},\dots,y_{k}\ddagger z_{k}}.

Let
